The adult who walks with a child, and who does not hurry the child along, learns to see the street as the child sees it, which is to say, as a place full of wonders, and not as a route between two points. The child asks questions, and the adult, who does not know the answers, is forced to look, and to wonder, and to admit that they do not know, and that is a good thing for both of them. The slow walk with a child is not a walk at all. It is a lesson in attention, taught by the one person in the party who has not yet learned to hurry.\n\nThe night walk has its own pleasures, and its own rules. At night, the familiar street becomes unfamiliar, and the walker must rely on other senses: the sound of footsteps, the smell of a garden, the light from a window, the shape of a roof against the sky. The night walk is best taken on a familiar route, and in a safe place, and it is best taken slowly, because the dark hides the uneven pavement and the low branch. But the reward is a street seen in a different light, quite literally, and a sky that is visible in a way it never is by day. The stars are there, above the rooftops, for anyone who looks up, and the slow walker, who has nowhere to be, is the one most likely to look up.\n\nThe slow walk is also the best cure for the screen, and the cure is simple. The screen asks for your attention and gives you the world in a rectangle. The walk asks for your attention and gives you the world itself, in three dimensions, with weather, and smell, and sound, and the chance of a conversation with a stranger. An hour of walking will not answer your emails, and it will not finish your work, and it will not make the news go away. But it will give your eyes a rest, and your mind a rest, and your body a little movement, and you will come home with a thought you did not have when you left, and that thought will be worth more than the hour you spent getting it.\n\nThe defence of the slow walk is, in the end, a defence of attention. The world is full of things worth looking at, and the slow walk is the way we look at them. It is the way we learn our own neighbourhoods, and the way we think our own thoughts, and the way we meet our neighbours, and the way we rest without stopping. It asks for nothing but an hour, and it gives back a street, and a thought, and a conversation, and a little peace. Walk slowly, and look around, and stop when something is worth stopping for. The world will still be there when you get home, and you will have seen a little more of it than you did before.\n"
import re, math
words = len(TEXT.split())
sentences = len([s for s in re.split(r'[.!?]+', TEXT) if s.strip()])
paragraphs = len([p for p in TEXT.split(chr(10)+chr(10)) if p.strip()])
reading_minutes = math.ceil(words / 200)
the_count = len(re.findall(r'\bthe\b', TEXT, re.IGNORECASE))

Full text

The Keeper of the Lighthouse Log By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

This portrait is a work of imagination. The keeper in it is fictional, and the lighthouse is fictional. The work described — the keeping of the light, the keeping of the log — is real work, done by real keepers in real lighthouses, and this portrait is drawn from the nature of that work.

The log is a book, bound in cloth, with pages ruled in faint blue lines, and it sits on a desk in the lantern room, beside the lamp, where the keeper can reach it without leaving the light. Every entry is written in the same hand, in ink, in a script that has grown smaller and neater over the years, as if the keeper had learned that the log is not a diary but a record, and that a record should be plain. The first entry, on the first page, is dated twenty-three years ago, and it says, in a hand that is larger and less certain: "Took up the light. Weather fair. Lamp lit at dusk. All well." The last entry, written last night, says: "Wind north-west, fresh. Sea rough. Lamp lit at dusk. All well." Between those two entries there are twenty-three years of weather, and of ships, and of nights, and the log holds them all.

The keeper is a man of sixty, with a face weathered by the wind that comes off the sea and up the tower, and with hands that are scarred from the work of the lamp, the small burns and nicks that come from handling hot glass and cold metal in the dark. He is not a talkative man, but he is not a silent one either. He talks when there is something to say, and he is silent when there is not, and he has learned, over twenty-three years, that most of the time there is not. He lives alone in the tower, except for the weeks when his relief comes, and he likes it that way, not because he dislikes people, but because the light does not keep office hours, and the work does not wait for company.

The work is simple to describe and hard to do well. The lamp must be lit at dusk, and it must burn until dawn, and it must not go out, and it must not dim, and it must turn, steadily, at the same speed, all night, every night, in all weathers. The keeper trims the wick, and cleans the lens, and winds the clockwork that turns the light, and watches the pressure of the lamp, and listens for the sound of the machinery, which he knows as well as he knows his own breathing, and which tells him, by a change in its note, that something is wrong before anything can be seen to be wrong. The work is a routine, and the routine is the point. A lighthouse does not need brilliance. It needs reliability, night after night, year after year, and reliability is a habit, kept by a person who does the same things, in the same order, every night, without fail.

The log is the other half of the work. Every night, the keeper writes down the weather: the wind, its direction and its strength, the state of the sea, the visibility, the cloud, the rain. He writes down the ships he has seen, and the time he saw them, and the direction they were going. He writes down anything unusual: a light where no light should be, a ship that does not answer the signal, a sound in the night that he cannot place. And he writes down the state of the lamp, and of the machinery, and of the tower, and of himself, in the plainest words he can find. The log is not written for himself. It is written for the person who will come after him, and for the office on shore that reads it, and for the inquiry that may one day need to know what the weather was on a particular night, at a particular hour, when a ship was lost. The keeper writes as if someone's life may depend on his entry, because it may.

The nights are long, and the keeper has learned to fill them without filling the log. He reads, in the hours when the lamp is burning well and the sea is quiet, and his books are the kind that can be picked up and put down: histories, and books of the sea, and a Bible that has been read so often its spine is broken. He listens to the radio, when the weather allows it, and he listens to the shipping forecast with the attention of a man whose work depends on it. He walks the gallery around the lantern, in all weathers, and looks at the sea, and at the sky, and at the land, and he learns the sea the way a farmer learns a field: by looking at it, day after day, until its moods are familiar.

The storms are the test, and the keeper does not pretend otherwise. In a storm, the tower shakes, and the wind screams around the lantern, and the rain drives against the glass, and the sea below is white with breaking water, and the light must still burn, and the keeper must still climb the stairs to tend it, holding the rail with both hands, with the tower moving under his feet. In the worst storm he has known, the sea came over the gallery, and the water ran down the stairs, and the keeper stood in the lantern room with his feet in water, keeping the lamp alight, and writing in the log, when the storm had passed, in a hand that shook a little: "Storm, severe. Sea over gallery. Lamp kept burning. All well." He does not talk about that night, except to say that the lamp did not go out, and that is the whole of the story, as far as he is concerned.

The loneliness is real, and the keeper does not deny it, but he does not complain about it either. He says that a lighthouse is not a lonely place, because the sea is always there, and the ships are always passing, and the light is always going out to them, and a man who keeps a light for strangers is not alone in the way that matters. He says that the worst of it is not the solitude but the waiting: the waiting for the relief boat, which comes when the weather allows and not before, and the waiting for news from shore, which comes in letters and in the occasional visitor, and the waiting for the night to end, on the longest nights of winter, when the lamp must burn for sixteen hours and the keeper must stay awake for all of them.

The relief keeper comes for a fortnight, twice a year, and the two men share the tower, and the work, and the log, and the relief keeper writes his entries in a different hand, a little larger, a little less neat, and the keeper, reading them when he returns, can tell from the entries what kind of fortnight it was: whether the weather was kind, whether the lamp behaved, whether the relief keeper was bored or busy. The handover is a ritual: the keeper shows the relief the state of the lamp, and the state of the stores, and the state of the log, and he tells him what to watch for, and then he goes ashore, and for a fortnight he is a man on land, and he walks in the town, and he buys what he needs, and he talks to people, and then he goes back to the tower, and the tower takes him back, as if he had never left.

The log, over twenty-three years, has become something more than a record. It is a history of the sea, written by one man, in one place, night after night, and it holds things that no other record holds: the night the wind changed in an hour and caught three fishing boats out; the week the fog lay so thick that the light could not be seen from the shore, and the keeper could hear the ships' horns but could not see them; the summer when the sea was so calm that the reflection of the light lay on the water like a road; the winter when the ice came down from the north and the sea was white with it. The log holds the weather, and the ships, and the lamp, and the keeper's life, all in the same plain hand, all in the same book, all written as if someone's life may depend on it.

The lamp itself has changed over the keeper's twenty-three years, and the keeper has changed with it. When he first took up the light, the lamp burned oil, and the keeper's work was the work of trimming wicks and filling tanks and watching the flame. Then the oil gave way to electricity, and the work became the work of bulbs and switches and generators, and the keeper learned the new machinery the way he had learned the old: by doing it, night after night, until it became habit. The lens, though, has not changed. It is the same great glass, cut into prisms, that has stood in the lantern room since the tower was built, gathering the light and throwing it out to sea in a beam that can be seen for twenty miles. The keeper cleans it every day, with a cloth and with care, because a dirty lens is a dim light, and a dim light is a broken promise.

The keeper's family lives on shore, and the letters come with the relief boat, and the keeper reads them in the lantern room, by the light of the lamp, and writes his replies at the same desk where he writes the log. His wife's letters tell him of the town, and of the children, and of the garden, and of the ordinary life that goes on without him, and his replies tell her of the sea, and of the weather, and of the ships, and of the ordinary life that goes on in the tower. The letters are not long, and they are not literary, but they are the thread that holds the keeper to the shore, and he keeps them in a box under the desk, tied in bundles by year, and he reads the old ones on the long nights of winter, when the lamp is burning well and there is nothing to do but wait for dawn.

There was a winter when the relief boat could not come, and the keeper stayed on the tower for nine weeks instead of six, and the stores ran low, and the fresh water ran lower, and the keeper rationed both, and kept the lamp burning, and wrote in the log, each night, in the same plain hand: "Relief delayed by weather. Stores low. Lamp lit at dusk. All well." When the boat finally came, the keeper went ashore for a fortnight, and he ate fresh bread, and he drank fresh water, and he slept in a bed that did not move, and then he went back to the tower, and the tower took him back. He does not tell this story as a story of hardship. He tells it as a story of the lamp not going out, which is, for him, the only story that matters.

The rock the tower stands on has its own life, and the keeper knows it. The gulls nest on the ledges in spring, and their cries fill the air, and the keeper, who has learned to live with them, does not curse them, though their droppings must be cleaned from the glass. The seals haul out on the rocks at low tide, and the keeper watches them through his glasses, and writes in the log when a pup is born, or when a storm drives them off. The tower is a small island of human work in a world of sea and stone, and the keeper is its only inhabitant, and he has learned to share it with the creatures that were there before him, and that will be there after he is gone.

One day, the keeper knows, the light will be automated, and the tower will be empty, and the log will be kept by a machine that never sleeps and never writes in ink. He does not resent it. He says that the light is what matters, and that a machine can keep a light, and that a machine will not get tired, and will not get lonely, and will not make a mistake because it was thinking of something else. But he says, too, that a machine will not write in the log that the sea was beautiful on a particular night, or that a ship passed close enough to see its name, or that the gulls came back in the spring. And he says that the log, when it is closed for the last time, will be a book that no machine could have written, because it is not only a record of the light. It is a record of a man who kept it, night after night, for twenty-three years, and who wrote, at the end of every night, in a hand that grew smaller and neater with the years: "All well."

He lives alone in the tower, except for the weeks when his relief comes, and he likes it that way, not because he dislikes people, but because the light does not keep office hours, and the work does not wait for company.\n\nThe work is simple to describe and hard to do well. The lamp must be lit at dusk, and it must burn until dawn, and it must not go out, and it must not dim, and it must turn, steadily, at the same speed, all night, every night, in all weathers. The keeper trims the wick, and cleans the lens, and winds the clockwork that turns the light, and watches the pressure of the lamp, and listens for the sound of the machinery, which he knows as well as he knows his own breathing, and which tells him, by a change in its note, that something is wrong before anything can be seen to be wrong. The work is a routine, and the routine is the point. A lighthouse does not need brilliance. It needs reliability, night after night, year after year, and reliability is a habit, kept by a person who does the same things, in the same order, every night, without fail.\n\nThe log is the other half of the work. Every night, the keeper writes down the weather: the wind, its direction and its strength, the state of the sea, the visibility, the cloud, the rain. He writes down the ships he has seen, and the time he saw them, and the direction they were going. He writes down anything unusual: a light where no light should be, a ship that does not answer the signal, a sound in the night that he cannot place. And he writes down the state of the lamp, and of the machinery, and of the tower, and of himself, in the plainest words he can find. The log is not written for himself. It is written for the person who will come after him, and for the office on shore that reads it, and for the inquiry that may one day need to know what the weather was on a particular night, at a particular hour, when a ship was lost. The keeper writes as if someone\'s life may depend on his entry, because it may.\n\nThe nights are long, and the keeper has learned to fill them without filling the log. He reads, in the hours when the lamp is burning well and the sea is quiet, and his books are the kind that can be picked up and put down: histories, and books of the sea, and a Bible that has been read so often its spine is broken. He listens to the radio, when the weather allows it, and he listens to the shipping forecast with the attention of a man whose work depends on it. He walks the gallery around the lantern, in all weathers, and looks at the sea, and at the sky, and at the land, and he learns the sea the way a farmer learns a field: by looking at it, day after day, until its moods are familiar.\n\nThe storms are the test, and the keeper does not pretend otherwise. In a storm, the tower shakes, and the wind screams around the lantern, and the rain drives against the glass, and the sea below is white with breaking water, and the light must still burn, and the keeper must still climb the stairs to tend it, holding the rail with both hands, with the tower moving under his feet. In the worst storm he has known, the sea came over the gallery, and the water ran down the stairs, and the keeper stood in the lantern room with his feet in water, keeping the lamp alight, and writing in the log, when the storm had passed, in a hand that shook a little: "Storm, severe. Sea over gallery. Lamp kept burning. All well." He does not talk about that night, except to say that the lamp did not go out, and that is the whole of the story, as far as he is concerned.\n\nThe loneliness is real, and the keeper does not deny it, but he does not complain about it either. He says that a lighthouse is not a lonely place, because the sea is always there, and the ships are always passing, and the light is always going out to them, and a man who keeps a light for strangers is not alone in the way that matters. He says that the worst of it is not the solitude but the waiting: the waiting for the relief boat, which comes when the weather allows and not before, and the waiting for news from shore, which comes in letters and in the occasional visitor, and the waiting for the night to end, on the longest nights of winter, when the lamp must burn for sixteen hours and the keeper must stay awake for all of them.\n\nThe relief keeper comes for a fortnight, twice a year, and the two men share the tower, and the work, and the log, and the relief keeper writes his entries in a different hand, a little larger, a little less neat, and the keeper, reading them when he returns, can tell from the entries what kind of fortnight it was: whether the weather was kind, whether the lamp behaved, whether the relief keeper was bored or busy. The handover is a ritual: the keeper shows the relief the state of the lamp, and the state of the stores, and the state of the log, and he tells him what to watch for, and then he goes ashore, and for a fortnight he is a man on land, and he walks in the town, and he buys what he needs, and he talks to people, and then he goes back to the tower, and the tower takes him back, as if he had never left.\n\nThe log, over twenty-three years, has become something more than a record. It is a history of the sea, written by one man, in one place, night after night, and it holds things that no other record holds: the night the wind changed in an hour and caught three fishing boats out; the week the fog lay so thick that the light could not be seen from the shore, and the keeper could hear the ships\' horns but could not see them; the summer when the sea was so calm that the reflection of the light lay on the water like a road; the winter when the ice came down from the north and the sea was white with it. The log holds the weather, and the ships, and the lamp, and the keeper\'s life, all in the same plain hand, all in the same book, all written as if someone\'s life may depend on it.\n\nThe lamp itself has changed over the keeper\'s twenty-three years, and the keeper has changed with it. When he first took up the light, the lamp burned oil, and the keeper\'s work was the work of trimming wicks and filling tanks and watching the flame. Then the oil gave way to electricity, and the work became the work of bulbs and switches and generators, and the keeper learned the new machinery the way he had learned the old: by doing it, night after night, until it became habit. The lens, though, has not changed. It is the same great glass, cut into prisms, that has stood in the lantern room since the tower was built, gathering the light and throwing it out to sea in a beam that can be seen for twenty miles. The keeper cleans it every day, with a cloth and with care, because a dirty lens is a dim light, and a dim light is a broken promise.\n\nThe keeper\'s family lives on shore, and the letters come with the relief boat, and the keeper reads them in the lantern room, by the light of the lamp, and writes his replies at the same desk where he writes the log. His wife\'s letters tell him of the town, and of the children, and of the garden, and of the ordinary life that goes on without him, and his replies tell her of the sea, and of the weather, and of the ships, and of the ordinary life that goes on in the tower. The letters are not long, and they are not literary, but they are the thread that holds the keeper to the shore, and he keeps them in a box under the desk, tied in bundles by year, and he reads the old ones on the long nights of winter, when the lamp is burning well and there is nothing to do but wait for dawn.\n\nThere was a winter when the relief boat could not come, and the keeper stayed on the tower for nine weeks instead of six, and the stores ran low, and the fresh water ran lower, and the keeper rationed both, and kept the lamp burning, and wrote in the log, each night, in the same plain hand: "Relief delayed by weather. Stores low. Lamp lit at dusk. All well." When the boat finally came, the keeper went ashore for a fortnight, and he ate fresh bread, and he drank fresh water, and he slept in a bed that did not move, and then he went back to the tower, and the tower took him back. He does not tell this story as a story of hardship. He tells it as a story of the lamp not going out, which is, for him, the only story that matters.\n\nThe rock the tower stands on has its own life, and the keeper knows it. The gulls nest on the ledges in spring, and their cries fill the air, and the keeper, who has learned to live with them, does not curse them, though their droppings must be cleaned from the glass. The seals haul out on the rocks at low tide, and the keeper watches them through his glasses, and writes in the log when a pup is born, or when a storm drives them off. The tower is a small island of human work in a world of sea and stone, and the keeper is its only inhabitant, and he has learned to share it with the creatures that were there before him, and that will be there after he is gone.\n\nOne day, the keeper knows, the light will be automated, and the tower will be empty, and the log will be kept by a machine that never sleeps and never writes in ink. He does not resent it. He says that the light is what matters, and that a machine can keep a light, and that a machine will not get tired, and will not get lonely, and will not make a mistake because it was thinking of something else. But he says, too, that a machine will not write in the log that the sea was beautiful on a particular night, or that a ship passed close enough to see its name, or that the gulls came back in the spring. And he says that the log, when it is closed for the last time, will be a book that no machine could have written, because it is not only a record of the light. It is a record of a man who kept it, night after night, for twenty-three years, and who wrote, at the end of every night, in a hand that grew smaller and neater with the years: "All well."\n'

Full text

How the Human Ear Hears a Whisper By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

A whisper is a very small thing. It is a breath of air, shaped by lips and tongue, carrying almost no energy at all, and yet it can cross a quiet room and be understood, word for word, by an ear that is listening. The ear does this with a piece of machinery so small it fits in the space behind your cheekbone, and so sensitive that it can hear a sound a thousand million times quieter than the loudest sound it can bear. How it does this is one of the finest stories in the body.

Sound, to begin with, is not a thing. It is a movement. When something speaks, or sings, or falls, it pushes the air, and the air pushes the air next to it, and the push travels outward in a wave, the way a ripple travels across a pond. The wave is a pattern of squeezing and stretching, of air pressed together and then pulled apart, and it travels at more than a thousand feet every second, which is why you hear a sound almost as soon as it is made. Your ear is a device for catching that wave, and for turning it into something your brain can understand.

The first part of the ear is the part you can see, the flap of skin and cartilage on the side of your head. It looks like a decoration, but it is a funnel, and its shape matters. It gathers the sound and guides it into the narrow canal that leads inward, and its folds and ridges shape the sound in a way that tells the brain where it came from. A sound from above strikes the ear differently from a sound from below, and a sound from behind differently from a sound from in front, and the brain, which has learned the shape of your own ears over a lifetime, uses those tiny differences to place a sound in space. That is why you can tell, with your eyes shut, whether someone is calling you from the left or the right, and whether they are above you or below you.

The canal is a tunnel about an inch long, and at the end of it is the eardrum, a thin skin stretched tight like the head of a drum. When the wave of sound reaches it, the eardrum moves, in and out, in time with the squeezing and stretching of the air. A loud sound moves it a lot. A whisper moves it almost not at all, by less than the width of an atom, and yet it moves, and the movement is enough. The eardrum is the first translator in the chain. It turns the movement of air into the movement of a membrane, and the membrane turns it into the movement of bone.

Behind the eardrum there is a small air-filled room, the middle ear, and in it are the three smallest bones in the human body. They have names that describe their shapes: the hammer, the anvil, and the stirrup. The hammer is joined to the eardrum, and the stirrup is joined to the inner ear, and the anvil sits between them, and together they form a lever. The lever does a clever thing. It takes the large, gentle movement of the eardrum and turns it into a small, forceful movement at the inner ear. The eardrum is large and the stirrup is small, so the same force is concentrated onto a smaller area, and the pressure is increased more than twenty times. This matters, because the inner ear is full of fluid, and fluid is much harder to move than air, and without the lever, most of the sound would bounce off the surface and never get in.

The middle ear also has a guard. When a sound is very loud, a tiny muscle pulls the stirrup away from the inner ear, and another tightens the eardrum, and the sound is damped before it can do damage. The guard is slow, and it cannot protect against a sudden bang, which is why a gunshot or a firecracker can hurt the ear before the muscle has time to act. But it can protect against a loud noise that builds up, and it is one of the reasons you can stand near a loudspeaker for a while without going deaf, though not for long, and not without cost.

The inner ear is where the real work happens, and it is a marvel. It is a coiled tube, shaped like a snail's shell, and it is called the cochlea, from the Latin for snail. Inside it there is fluid, and along its length there is a membrane, and on the membrane there are thousands of tiny hair cells, each with a tuft of hairs on its top. When the stirrup pushes on the fluid, the fluid moves, and the membrane ripples, and the ripple travels along the coil, and the hair cells bend. The bending opens a tiny gate in the cell, and an electrical signal is sent to the brain. That is hearing: the bending of a hair, no thicker than a thread, by a wave of fluid set in motion by a bone the size of a grain of rice, set in motion by a membrane moved by the air.

The coil is not uniform, and that is the secret of how the ear tells one pitch from another. The membrane is stiff and narrow at one end of the coil, near the stirrup, and wide and floppy at the other end, far from it. A high note, with its fast vibration, makes the stiff end ripple. A low note, with its slow vibration, makes the floppy end ripple. Each place along the coil is tuned to a different pitch, like the keys of a piano, and the brain knows which pitch it is hearing by knowing which place on the coil is rippling. A whisper, which is a complicated sound made of many pitches at once, sets many places rippling at once, and the brain reads the pattern, and hears a word.

The hair cells are the most delicate part of the whole machine, and they are the part that cannot be repaired. There are about sixteen thousand of them in each ear, and once they are damaged, they do not grow back. Loud noise damages them, and age damages them, and the damage begins at the high end, with the highest pitches, which is why an older person can hear a low voice but cannot hear a child's voice, or the consonants that make speech clear. The whisper, which lives in those high, quiet pitches, is the first thing to go. A person who has lost their hair cells has not lost their ears. They have lost the instrument inside them, and no hearing aid can replace it, though a hearing aid can amplify what is left.

The ear also does a second job, one that has nothing to do with hearing, and it does it in the same small space. Beside the cochlea there are three small loops, set at right angles to each other, filled with fluid, and lined with hair cells of their own. These are the organs of balance. When you turn your head, the fluid in the loops moves, and the hair cells bend, and the brain knows which way you have turned. When you stand still, the fluid settles, and the brain knows you are still. The ear is not only the organ of hearing. It is the organ of knowing where you are in space, and of keeping you upright, and a person whose balance organs are disturbed will feel dizzy, and the world will seem to spin, because the ear is telling the brain a lie about which way is up.

The brain does the last part of the work, and it is the largest part. The electrical signals from the ear travel along a nerve to the brain, and the brain does with them what it does with everything: it makes sense of them. It separates one voice from another in a crowded room, and it does so by listening for the differences in pitch and timing and direction, and by filling in the gaps from memory. It hears a word it knows, even when half the word is missing, because it knows what word ought to be there. It hears a whisper in a quiet room, and it turns its whole attention to it, because a whisper is a sound that asks to be listened to. The ear collects the sound. The brain hears it.

The ear is also a social organ, and it is worth saying so, because the science of it can make it sound like a machine, and it is not only a machine. It is the organ by which we hear each other's voices, and by which we know each other's moods, and by which a child learns to speak, by hearing speech and imitating it. A child who cannot hear cannot learn to speak in the ordinary way, and must learn by other means, and the loss is not only of sound but of connection. The ear is how we are joined to each other, by voice, and by music, and by the small sounds of a house: the kettle, the footstep, the voice calling from another room.

There is a quietness to the whisper that is worth noticing. A whisper is a sound made without the voice, by air alone, shaped by the mouth into words. It carries almost no power, and it travels only a short way, and it is meant for one listener, close by. The ear that hears it is doing something remarkable: it is taking a movement of air so small that it barely disturbs the dust, and it is turning it into meaning. The whole chain, from the funnel of the outer ear to the lever of the middle ear to the rippling membrane of the cochlea to the bending of a hair to the firing of a nerve to the understanding of the brain, is a chain of translations, each one faithful, each one necessary, and each one smaller than the last.

And the chain is fragile. It can be broken by a loud noise, by an infection, by age, by a blow, and when it is broken, the world goes quiet, and the quiet is not peaceful. It is isolating, and it is frightening, and it is lonely, because the ear is how we know that others are near. The care of the ear is simple, and it is mostly a matter of not abusing it: not standing too close to the loudspeaker, not listening to music at full volume through earphones, not poking at the canal with anything, and seeing a doctor when the ear hurts or when the hearing changes. The ear will last a lifetime if it is treated with respect, and it will repay that respect every day, with the sound of a voice, and the sound of music, and the sound of a whisper in a quiet room.

Music is the ear's finest test, and its finest reward. A single note from a violin is not a single sound but a crowd of sounds, a fundamental pitch with a family of overtones above it, and the ear separates them, and hears them as one rich note, and tells a violin from a flute playing the same pitch by the pattern of the overtones alone. A chord is several notes at once, each setting its own place on the coil rippling, and the brain hears them together, and hears the harmony, and the dissonance, and the resolution. No machine made by human hands can hear music the way an ear does, because no machine has a brain behind it that knows what music means.

That is how the human ear hears a whisper: by catching a wave of air in a funnel, by passing it through a drum and a lever of three small bones, by setting a fluid in motion in a coiled tube, by bending a hair no thicker than a thread, and by sending the signal to a brain that knows what the whisper means. It is a small machine, and it is a quiet one, and it works, night and day, without rest, for a lifetime, turning the air into meaning. Listen to it, and look after it, and it will listen for you.

The whole chain, from the funnel of the outer ear to the lever of the middle ear to the rippling membrane of the cochlea to the bending of a hair to the firing of a nerve to the understanding of the brain, is a chain of translations, each one faithful, each one necessary, and each one smaller than the last.\n\nAnd the chain is fragile. It can be broken by a loud noise, by an infection, by age, by a blow, and when it is broken, the world goes quiet, and the quiet is not peaceful. It is isolating, and it is frightening, and it is lonely, because the ear is how we know that others are near. The care of the ear is simple, and it is mostly a matter of not abusing it: not standing too close to the loudspeaker, not listening to music at full volume through earphones, not poking at the canal with anything, and seeing a doctor when the ear hurts or when the hearing changes. The ear will last a lifetime if it is treated with respect, and it will repay that respect every day, with the sound of a voice, and the sound of music, and the sound of a whisper in a quiet room.\n\nMusic is the ear's finest test, and its finest reward. A single note from a violin is not a single sound but a crowd of sounds, a fundamental pitch with a family of overtones above it, and the ear separates them, and hears them as one rich note, and tells a violin from a flute playing the same pitch by the pattern of the overtones alone. A chord is several notes at once, each setting its own place on the coil rippling, and the brain hears them together, and hears the harmony, and the dissonance, and the resolution. No machine made by human hands can hear music the way an ear does, because no machine has a brain behind it that knows what music means.\n\nThat is how the human ear hears a whisper: by catching a wave of air in a funnel, by passing it through a drum and a lever of three small bones, by setting a fluid in motion in a coiled tube, by bending a hair no thicker than a thread, and by sending the signal to a brain that knows what the whisper means. It is a small machine, and it is a quiet one, and it works, night and day, without rest, for a lifetime, turning the air into meaning. Listen to it, and look after it, and it will listen for you.\n"

# Program: How the Human Ear Hears a Whisper
TEXT = "How the Human Ear Hears a Whisper\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nA whisper is a very small thing. It is a breath of air, shaped by lips and tongue, carrying almost no energy at all, and yet it can cross a quiet room and be understood, word for word, by an ear that is listening. The ear does this with a piece of machinery so small it fits in the space behind your cheekbone, and so sensitive that it can hear a sound a thousand million times quieter than the loudest sound it can bear. How it does this is one of the finest stories in the body.\n\nSound, to begin with, is not a thing. It is a movement. When something speaks, or sings, or falls, it pushes the air, and the air pushes the air next to it, and the push travels outward in a wave, the way a ripple travels across a pond. The wave is a pattern of squeezing and stretching, of air pressed together and then pulled apart, and it travels at more than a thousand feet every second, which is why you hear a sound almost as soon as it is made. Your ear is a device for catching that wave, and for turning it into something your brain can understand.\n\nThe first part of the ear is the part you can see, the flap of skin and cartilage on the side of your head. It looks like a decoration, but it is a funnel, and its shape matters. It gathers the sound and guides it into the narrow canal that leads inward, and its folds and ridges shape the sound in a way that tells the brain where it came from. A sound from above strikes the ear differently from a sound from below, and a sound from behind differently from a sound from in front, and the brain, which has learned the shape of your own ears over a lifetime, uses those tiny differences to place a sound in space. That is why you can tell, with your eyes shut, whether someone is calling you from the left or the right, and whether they are above you or below you.\n\nThe canal is a tunnel about an inch long, and at the end of it is the eardrum, a thin skin stretched tight like the head of a drum. When the wave of sound reaches it, the eardrum moves, in and out, in time with the squeezing and stretching of the air. A loud sound moves it a lot. A whisper moves it almost not at all, by less than the width of an atom, and yet it moves, and the movement is enough. The eardrum is the first translator in the chain. It turns the movement of air into the movement of a membrane, and the membrane turns it into the movement of bone.\n\nBehind the eardrum there is a small air-filled room, the middle ear, and in it are the three smallest bones in the human body. They have names that describe their shapes: the hammer, the anvil, and the stirrup. The hammer is joined to the eardrum, and the stirrup is joined to the inner ear, and the anvil sits between them, and together they form a lever. The lever does a clever thing. It takes the large, gentle movement of the eardrum and turns it into a small, forceful movement at the inner ear. The eardrum is large and the stirrup is small, so the same force is concentrated onto a smaller area, and the pressure is increased more than twenty times. This matters, because the inner ear is full of fluid, and fluid is much harder to move than air, and without the lever, most of the sound would bounce off the surface and never get in.\n\nThe middle ear also has a guard. When a sound is very loud, a tiny muscle pulls the stirrup away from the inner ear, and another tightens the eardrum, and the sound is damped before it can do damage. The guard is slow, and it cannot protect against a sudden bang, which is why a gunshot or a firecracker can hurt the ear before the muscle has time to act. But it can protect against a loud noise that builds up, and it is one of the reasons you can stand near a loudspeaker for a while without going deaf, though not for long, and not without cost.\n\nThe inner ear is where the real work happens, and it is a marvel. It is a coiled tube, shaped like a snail's shell, and it is called the cochlea, from the Latin for snail. Inside it there is fluid, and along its length there is a membrane, and on the membrane there are thousands of tiny hair cells, each with a tuft of hairs on its top. When the stirrup pushes on the fluid, the fluid moves, and the membrane ripples, and the ripple travels along the coil, and the hair cells bend. The bending opens a tiny gate in the cell, and an electrical signal is sent to the brain. That is hearing: the bending of a hair, no thicker than a thread, by a wave of fluid set in motion by a bone the size of a grain of rice, set in motion by a membrane moved by the air.\n\nThe coil is not uniform, and that is the secret of how the ear tells one pitch from another. The membrane is stiff and narrow at one end of the coil, near the stirrup, and wide and floppy at the other end, far from it. A high note, with its fast vibration, makes the stiff end ripple. A low note, with its slow vibration, makes the floppy end ripple. Each place along the coil is tuned to a different pitch, like the keys of a piano, and the brain knows which pitch it is hearing by knowing which place on the coil is rippling. A whisper, which is a complicated sound made of many pitches at once, sets many places rippling at once, and the brain reads the pattern, and hears a word.\n\nThe hair cells are the most delicate part of the whole machine, and they are the part that cannot be repaired. There are about sixteen thousand of them in each ear, and once they are damaged, they do not grow back. Loud noise damages them, and age damages them, and the damage begins at the high end, with the highest pitches, which is why an older person can hear a low voice but cannot hear a child's voice, or the consonants that make speech clear. The whisper, which lives in those high, quiet pitches, is the first thing to go. A person who has lost their hair cells has not lost their ears. They have lost the instrument inside them, and no hearing aid can replace it, though a hearing aid can amplify what is left.\n\nThe ear also does a second job, one that has nothing to do with hearing, and it does it in the same small space. Beside the cochlea there are three small loops, set at right angles to each other, filled with fluid, and lined with hair cells of their own. These are the organs of balance. When you turn your head, the fluid in the loops moves, and the hair cells bend, and the brain knows which way you have turned. When you stand still, the fluid settles, and the brain knows you are still. The ear is not only the organ of hearing. It is the organ of knowing where you are in space, and of keeping you upright, and a person whose balance organs are disturbed will feel dizzy, and the world will seem to spin, because the ear is telling the brain a lie about which way is up.\n\nThe brain does the last part of the work, and it is the largest part. The electrical signals from the ear travel along a nerve to the brain, and the brain does with them what it does with everything: it makes sense of them. It separates one voice from another in a crowded room, and it does so by listening for the differences in pitch and timing and direction, and by filling in the gaps from memory. It hears a word it knows, even when half the word is missing, because it knows what word ought to be there. It hears a whisper in a quiet room, and it turns its whole attention to it, because a whisper is a sound that asks to be listened to. The ear collects the sound. The brain hears it.\n\nThe ear is also a social organ, and it is worth saying so, because the science of it can make it sound like a machine, and it is not only a machine. It is the organ by which we hear each other's voices, and by which we know each other's moods, and by which a child learns to speak, by hearing speech and imitating it. A child who cannot hear cannot learn to speak in the ordinary way, and must learn by other means, and the loss is not only of sound but of connection. The ear is how we are joined to each other, by voice, and by music, and by the small sounds of a house: the kettle, the footstep, the voice calling from another room.\n\nThere is a quietness to the whisper that is worth noticing. A whisper is a sound made without the voice, by air alone, shaped by the mouth into words. It carries almost no power, and it travels only a short way, and it is meant for one listener, close by. The ear that hears it is doing something remarkable: it is taking a movement of air so small that it barely disturbs the dust, and it is turning it into meaning. The whole chain, from the funnel of the outer ear to the lever of the middle ear to the rippling membrane of the cochlea to the bending of a hair to the firing of a nerve to the understanding of the brain, is a chain of translations, each one faithful, each one necessary, and each one smaller than the last.\n\nAnd the chain is fragile. It can be broken by a loud noise, by an infection, by age, by a blow, and when it is broken, the world goes quiet, and the quiet is not peaceful. It is isolating, and it is frightening, and it is lonely, because the ear is how we know that others are near. The care of the ear is simple, and it is mostly a matter of not abusing it: not standing too close to the loudspeaker, not listening to music at full volume through earphones, not poking at the canal with anything, and seeing a doctor when the ear hurts or when the hearing changes. The ear will last a lifetime if it is treated with respect, and it will repay that respect every day, with the sound of a voice, and the sound of music, and the sound of a whisper in a quiet room.\n\nMusic is the ear's finest test, and its finest reward. A single note from a violin is not a single sound but a crowd of sounds, a fundamental pitch with a family of overtones above it, and the ear separates them, and hears them as one rich note, and tells a violin from a flute playing the same pitch by the pattern of the overtones alone. A chord is several notes at once, each setting its own place on the coil rippling, and the brain hears them together, and hears the harmony, and the dissonance, and the resolution. No machine made by human hands can hear music the way an ear does, because no machine has a brain behind it that knows what music means.\n\nThat is how the human ear hears a whisper: by catching a wave of air in a funnel, by passing it through a drum and a lever of three small bones, by setting a fluid in motion in a coiled tube, by bending a hair no thicker than a thread, and by sending the signal to a brain that knows what the whisper means. It is a small machine, and it is a quiet one, and it works, night and day, without rest, for a lifetime, turning the air into meaning. Listen to it, and look after it, and it will listen for you.\n"

Full text

Let the Streets Belong to People Again By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Walk down the main street of almost any town, and you will see the same thing: a street that belongs to cars. The road is wide, the pavements are narrow, the crossings are few, and the pedestrian, if there is one, is hurrying along the edge, pressed against the shop fronts, waiting for a gap in the traffic that never quite comes. The street was built for people, and it has been given away to machines, and the giving away happened so gradually that nobody noticed it happening, and now it seems natural, as if a street were a place for cars and people were merely visitors in it.

It was not always so. For most of human history, a street was a place where people walked, and talked, and traded, and played, and where a cart or a carriage passed through, slowly, and with deference to the people on foot. The street was a room of the town, open to the sky, and it belonged to everyone who used it. The coming of the car changed that, and it changed it fast. The car was faster, and heavier, and more dangerous than anything that had used the street before, and the street was remade to suit it: widened, straightened, cleared of obstacles, and fenced off from the people who had once owned it. The pedestrian was given a narrow strip at the edge, and told to stay on it, and the street became a road, and the road became a place for cars.

The cost of that change is paid every day, in ways that are easy to miss because they are so ordinary. A child cannot walk to school alone, because the road is too dangerous, so the child is driven, and the driving adds another car to the road, and the road becomes more dangerous still. An old person cannot cross the street to the shops, because the crossing is too far away and the lights change too fast, so the old person stays at home, and the staying at home becomes loneliness. A shopkeeper watches the cars go past and wonders why nobody stops, not realising that a street full of fast cars is a street nobody wants to linger in, and that a shop needs lingerers, not passers-by. The street that belongs to cars is a street that serves nobody well, not even the drivers, who sit in traffic on a road that was built to be driven on and find that it cannot be driven on, because there are too many cars on it.

The answer is not to ban the car. The car is a useful machine, and it has its place, and its place is on the road between towns, and on the edge of the town, and in the streets where it is genuinely needed. The answer is to give the street back to the people who live on it, and to the people who walk on it, and to make the car a guest in the street, instead of its owner. This is not a new idea. It is an old idea, and it has been tried, in towns all over the world, and where it has been tried, it has worked.

The first step is the simplest, and it is the hardest to argue against: make the pavements wide, and the road narrow, and let the pedestrian have the room that the car has taken. A wide pavement is not a luxury. It is a place where people can walk side by side, and where a pushchair can pass a wheelchair, and where a shop can put out a table, and where a tree can grow. A narrow road is not a punishment for drivers. It is a reminder that the street is a shared place, and that the car is passing through it, and that it should pass through slowly, and with care.

The second step is to slow the traffic down. A car travelling at thirty miles an hour is a danger to everyone on foot. A car travelling at twenty is a danger still, but a smaller one, and a car travelling at ten, in a street that is designed for ten, is a guest, and can be lived with. The speed of a street is set not by the signs but by the design: by the width of the road, by the presence of trees and benches and crossings, by the feeling that this is a place where people live, and not a road that happens to have houses on it. A street that looks like a place for people will be driven through slowly. A street that looks like a road will be driven through fast, whatever the signs say.

The third step is to give the street back its crossings, and to give them to the people, not to the cars. A crossing should be where people want to cross, not where it is convenient for the traffic. It should be level with the pavement, so that a wheelchair, and a pushchair, and an old person with a stick, can cross without stepping down and up again. It should be short, so that the crossing does not take longer than the lights allow. And it should be frequent, so that nobody has to walk a hundred yards out of their way to cross their own street. A street with crossings every few dozen yards is a street that can be lived in. A street with one crossing at the end is a street that has been divided in two.

The fourth step is to give the street its trees, and its benches, and its places to sit. A street without a bench is a street that says: do not stay. A street with a bench says: sit a while. A street with trees says: this is a place to be, not only a place to pass through. The tree gives shade in summer, and colour in autumn, and a home for birds, and a reason to look up. The bench gives a rest to the old, and a meeting place to the young, and a place for the tired to sit. These are small things, and they cost little, and they change a street more than any amount of resurfacing.

The fifth step is to let the shops spill out, a little, onto the pavement. A shop with a table outside, and a few chairs, and a display of fruit, is a shop that is part of the street, and it makes the street a place where people want to be. A shop behind a blank window, with a sign telling you to keep moving, is a shop that has given up on the street. The street that belongs to people is a street where the shops are open to it, and where the pavement is a place to linger, and where a person can stop, and look, and buy, and talk, without being in anyone's way.

There are objections, and they are always the same, and they deserve an honest answer. The first is that the traffic has to go somewhere, and that slowing it down in the main street will only move the congestion elsewhere. This is true, and it is also the point. The traffic that is passing through a town, on its way to somewhere else, should be on a road built for it, around the town, not through it. The traffic that is coming into the town, to shop, or to work, or to visit, will come anyway, and it will come more slowly, and it will park, and the driver will walk, and the walking will be pleasant, and the driver will come back. A town that is easy to walk in is a town that people come back to. A town that is easy to drive through is a town that people drive through, and do not stop.

The second objection is that the shops will suffer, because the drivers will not be able to park outside them. The evidence says the opposite. The shops that suffer are the shops on streets that are unpleasant to walk in, because nobody lingers, and nobody lingers because the street is full of fast cars. The shops that thrive are the shops on streets where people walk, and linger, and look, and buy. A person on foot spends more, over a year, in the shops of their own street, than a person in a car spends in the shops they drive past. The car is a poor customer. The pedestrian is a good one, and the pedestrian needs a street worth walking in.

The third objection is that it costs money, and that towns cannot afford it. It costs money to widen a pavement, and to plant a tree, and to put in a bench, and to narrow a road. But it costs more, over time, not to do it. It costs in the health of the people who cannot walk, and who drive instead, and who grow unwell for want of exercise. It costs in the loneliness of the old, who cannot cross the street, and who stay at home. It costs in the shops that close, and the streets that empty, and the towns that become places people drive through, and do not stop. The street that belongs to people is not a luxury. It is an investment, and it pays for itself in health, and in trade, and in the life of the town.

The children are the test of a street, and they are the most honest test there is. A street where children play is a street that is safe, and slow, and watched by neighbours, and a street where children do not play is a street that has failed, whatever its traffic figures say. The child who plays in the street is learning the street, and learning the neighbourhood, and learning to be a citizen of it, in a way that no amount of being driven through it can teach. The street that belongs to people is a street where a ball can be kicked, and a chalk drawing can be made on the pavement, and a child can ride a bicycle, slowly, in safety, and where the drivers, when they come, come slowly, and expect children, and drive as if children mattered. Because they do.

The market is the street at its best, and it is worth a paragraph, because it shows what a street can be when it is given back to people. A market street, on market day, is closed to traffic, and the stalls go up, and the street fills with people, and with smells, and with noise, and with the ordinary business of buying and selling, and of meeting, and of talking. The market is old, older than the car, older than the town, and it is the reason the street exists at all: a place where people come together to trade, and where the town shows itself to itself. A town that has a market street has a heart. A town that has given its market street to the car has lost it.

And the sound of a good street is worth listening for. A good street sounds like footsteps, and conversation, and a shop bell, and a child laughing, and a bird in a tree, and the low murmur of people going about their business. A bad street sounds like engines, and horns, and brakes, and the rush of tyres on tarmac, and nothing else. The ear knows the difference, even when the eye does not, and the street that belongs to people is a street that sounds like people. Stand in a street and close your eyes, and listen, and you will know, in a moment, whether the street belongs to the people who live on it, or to the machines that pass through it.

The street belongs to the people who live on it, and who walk on it, and who shop on it, and who sit on its benches, and who plant its trees. It was taken from them, slowly, over a century, by a machine that is useful but that does not belong in the middle of a town. It can be given back, slowly, over a decade, by widening the pavement, and slowing the traffic, and putting in the crossings, and planting the trees, and letting the shops spill out. The car will still have its place, on the road between towns, and on the edge of the town, and in the streets where it is needed. But the street, the main street, the street where people live, should belong to people again. Let it belong to people again.

The speed of a street is set not by the signs but by the design: by the width of the road, by the presence of trees and benches and crossings, by the feeling that this is a place where people live, and not a road that happens to have houses on it. A street that looks like a place for people will be driven through slowly. A street that looks like a road will be driven through fast, whatever the signs say.\n\nThe third step is to give the street back its crossings, and to give them to the people, not to the cars. A crossing should be where people want to cross, not where it is convenient for the traffic. It should be level with the pavement, so that a wheelchair, and a pushchair, and an old person with a stick, can cross without stepping down and up again. It should be short, so that the crossing does not take longer than the lights allow. And it should be frequent, so that nobody has to walk a hundred yards out of their way to cross their own street. A street with crossings every few dozen yards is a street that can be lived in. A street with one crossing at the end is a street that has been divided in two.\n\nThe fourth step is to give the street its trees, and its benches, and its places to sit. A street without a bench is a street that says: do not stay. A street with a bench says: sit a while. A street with trees says: this is a place to be, not only a place to pass through. The tree gives shade in summer, and colour in autumn, and a home for birds, and a reason to look up. The bench gives a rest to the old, and a meeting place to the young, and a place for the tired to sit. These are small things, and they cost little, and they change a street more than any amount of resurfacing.\n\nThe fifth step is to let the shops spill out, a little, onto the pavement. A shop with a table outside, and a few chairs, and a display of fruit, is a shop that is part of the street, and it makes the street a place where people want to be. A shop behind a blank window, with a sign telling you to keep moving, is a shop that has given up on the street. The street that belongs to people is a street where the shops are open to it, and where the pavement is a place to linger, and where a person can stop, and look, and buy, and talk, without being in anyone's way.\n\nThere are objections, and they are always the same, and they deserve an honest answer. The first is that the traffic has to go somewhere, and that slowing it down in the main street will only move the congestion elsewhere. This is true, and it is also the point. The traffic that is passing through a town, on its way to somewhere else, should be on a road built for it, around the town, not through it. The traffic that is coming into the town, to shop, or to work, or to visit, will come anyway, and it will come more slowly, and it will park, and the driver will walk, and the walking will be pleasant, and the driver will come back. A town that is easy to walk in is a town that people come back to. A town that is easy to drive through is a town that people drive through, and do not stop.\n\nThe second objection is that the shops will suffer, because the drivers will not be able to park outside them. The evidence says the opposite. The shops that suffer are the shops on streets that are unpleasant to walk in, because nobody lingers, and nobody lingers because the street is full of fast cars. The shops that thrive are the shops on streets where people walk, and linger, and look, and buy. A person on foot spends more, over a year, in the shops of their own street, than a person in a car spends in the shops they drive past. The car is a poor customer. The pedestrian is a good one, and the pedestrian needs a street worth walking in.\n\nThe third objection is that it costs money, and that towns cannot afford it. It costs money to widen a pavement, and to plant a tree, and to put in a bench, and to narrow a road. But it costs more, over time, not to do it. It costs in the health of the people who cannot walk, and who drive instead, and who grow unwell for want of exercise. It costs in the loneliness of the old, who cannot cross the street, and who stay at home. It costs in the shops that close, and the streets that empty, and the towns that become places people drive through, and do not stop. The street that belongs to people is not a luxury. It is an investment, and it pays for itself in health, and in trade, and in the life of the town.\n\nThe children are the test of a street, and they are the most honest test there is. A street where children play is a street that is safe, and slow, and watched by neighbours, and a street where children do not play is a street that has failed, whatever its traffic figures say. The child who plays in the street is learning the street, and learning the neighbourhood, and learning to be a citizen of it, in a way that no amount of being driven through it can teach. The street that belongs to people is a street where a ball can be kicked, and a chalk drawing can be made on the pavement, and a child can ride a bicycle, slowly, in safety, and where the drivers, when they come, come slowly, and expect children, and drive as if children mattered. Because they do.\n\nThe market is the street at its best, and it is worth a paragraph, because it shows what a street can be when it is given back to people. A market street, on market day, is closed to traffic, and the stalls go up, and the street fills with people, and with smells, and with noise, and with the ordinary business of buying and selling, and of meeting, and of talking. The market is old, older than the car, older than the town, and it is the reason the street exists at all: a place where people come together to trade, and where the town shows itself to itself. A town that has a market street has a heart. A town that has given its market street to the car has lost it.\n\nAnd the sound of a good street is worth listening for. A good street sounds like footsteps, and conversation, and a shop bell, and a child laughing, and a bird in a tree, and the low murmur of people going about their business. A bad street sounds like engines, and horns, and brakes, and the rush of tyres on tarmac, and nothing else. The ear knows the difference, even when the eye does not, and the street that belongs to people is a street that sounds like people. Stand in a street and close your eyes, and listen, and you will know, in a moment, whether the street belongs to the people who live on it, or to the machines that pass through it.\n\nThe street belongs to the people who live on it, and who walk on it, and who shop on it, and who sit on its benches, and who plant its trees. It was taken from them, slowly, over a century, by a machine that is useful but that does not belong in the middle of a town. It can be given back, slowly, over a decade, by widening the pavement, and slowing the traffic, and putting in the crossings, and planting the trees, and letting the shops spill out. The car will still have its place, on the road between towns, and on the edge of the town, and in the streets where it is needed. But the street, the main street, the street where people live, should belong to people again. Let it belong to people again.\n"

TEXT = "Let the Streets Belong to People Again\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nWalk down the main street of almost any town, and you will see the same thing: a street that belongs to cars. The road is wide, the pavements are narrow, the crossings are few, and the pedestrian, if there is one, is hurrying along the edge, pressed against the shop fronts, waiting for a gap in the traffic that never quite comes. The street was built for people, and it has been given away to machines, and the giving away happened so gradually that nobody noticed it happening, and now it seems natural, as if a street were a place for cars and people were merely visitors in it.\n\nIt was not always so. For most of human history, a street was a place where people walked, and talked, and traded, and played, and where a cart or a carriage passed through, slowly, and with deference to the people on foot. The street was a room of the town, open to the sky, and it belonged to everyone who used it. The coming of the car changed that, and it changed it fast. The car was faster, and heavier, and more dangerous than anything that had used the street before, and the street was remade to suit it: widened, straightened, cleared of obstacles, and fenced off from the people who had once owned it. The pedestrian was given a narrow strip at the edge, and told to stay on it, and the street became a road, and the road became a place for cars.\n\nThe cost of that change is paid every day, in ways that are easy to miss because they are so ordinary. A child cannot walk to school alone, because the road is too dangerous, so the child is driven, and the driving adds another car to the road, and the road becomes more dangerous still. An old person cannot cross the street to the shops, because the crossing is too far away and the lights change too fast, so the old person stays at home, and the staying at home becomes loneliness. A shopkeeper watches the cars go past and wonders why nobody stops, not realising that a street full of fast cars is a street nobody wants to linger in, and that a shop needs lingerers, not passers-by. The street that belongs to cars is a street that serves nobody well, not even the drivers, who sit in traffic on a road that was built to be driven on and find that it cannot be driven on, because there are too many cars on it.\n\nThe answer is not to ban the car. The car is a useful machine, and it has its place, and its place is on the road between towns, and on the edge of the town, and in the streets where it is genuinely needed. The answer is to give the street back to the people who live on it, and to the people who walk on it, and to make the car a guest in the street, instead of its owner. This is not a new idea. It is an old idea, and it has been tried, in towns all over the world, and where it has been tried, it has worked.\n\nThe first step is the simplest, and it is the hardest to argue against: make the pavements wide, and the road narrow, and let the pedestrian have the room that the car has taken. A wide pavement is not a luxury. It is a place where people can walk side by side, and where a pushchair can pass a wheelchair, and where a shop can put out a table, and where a tree can grow. A narrow road is not a punishment for drivers. It is a reminder that the street is a shared place, and that the car is passing through it, and that it should pass through slowly, and with care.\n\nThe second step is to slow the traffic down. A car travelling at thirty miles an hour is a danger to everyone on foot. A car travelling at twenty is a danger still, but a smaller one, and a car travelling at ten, in a street that is designed for ten, is a guest, and can be lived with. The speed of a street is set not by the signs but by the design: by the width of the road, by the presence of trees and benches and crossings, by the feeling that this is a place where people live, and not a road that happens to have houses on it. A street that looks like a place for people will be driven through slowly. A street that looks like a road will be driven through fast, whatever the signs say.\n\nThe third step is to give the street back its crossings, and to give them to the people, not to the cars. A crossing should be where people want to cross, not where it is convenient for the traffic. It should be level with the pavement, so that a wheelchair, and a pushchair, and an old person with a stick, can cross without stepping down and up again. It should be short, so that the crossing does not take longer than the lights allow. And it should be frequent, so that nobody has to walk a hundred yards out of their way to cross their own street. A street with crossings every few dozen yards is a street that can be lived in. A street with one crossing at the end is a street that has been divided in two.\n\nThe fourth step is to give the street its trees, and its benches, and its places to sit. These are small things, and they cost little, and they change a street more than any amount of resurfacing.\n\nThe fifth step is to let the shops spill out, a little, onto the pavement.
