Full text

The Night Shift at the Weather Station By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

The weather station in this article is an illustrative one, drawn from the ordinary practices of weather stations in many places. No single real station is described, and no real observer is named.

The night shift begins at ten o'clock, when the day observer goes home and the night observer takes the keys. The station is a small building on a hill, with a fenced enclosure of instruments outside, and a view, on a clear night, of the town below, with its lights, and of the sky above, with its stars. The night observer is a woman of forty, who has worked the night shift for six years, and who likes it, not because she dislikes people, but because the night is when the weather shows itself most plainly, and because the station, at night, belongs to her.

The first duty is the ten o'clock observation, and it is done the same way it has been done for a hundred years. The observer goes out to the enclosure, with a torch, and reads the instruments: the thermometer in its white shelter, the rain gauge, the wind vane and the anemometer on their mast, the barometer in the room, and the hygrometer that measures the dampness of the air. She writes the readings in the log, in the same columns, in the same order, every night, because the log is a record that must be comparable, night to night, year to year, and a record is only comparable if it is kept the same way. The readings take ten minutes, and they are the foundation of everything the station does.

Then there is the sky. The observer looks up, and writes down the cloud: how much of the sky it covers, what kind it is, which way it is moving, and how fast. On a clear night, she writes down the stars, and the moon, and the visibility, which is how far she can see, judged by the lights of the town and the hills beyond it. On a cloudy night, she writes down the cloud base, judged by the way the cloud hides the hilltop, or does not. The sky is the part of the observation that cannot be read from an instrument, and it is the part that needs a human eye, and a human judgement, and a human memory of what the sky looked like last night, and the night before.

The barometer is the instrument the observer watches most closely, because it is the one that tells the future. The barometer measures the pressure of the air, and the pressure is falling, or rising, or steady, and the falling or rising tells the observer what the weather is doing, and what it will do next. A falling barometer means the weather is coming in, and a rising barometer means it is going out, and a barometer that falls fast means a storm, and a barometer that rises fast means a clearing. The observer has learned to read the barometer the way a sailor reads the sea, and she can tell, from the way it has moved over the last three hours, whether the rain will come before dawn, or after it, or not at all.

The rain gauge is a simple thing, a cylinder with a funnel, and it is read by pouring the water into a measure, and writing down the depth. The rain that falls at night is measured in the morning, and the rain that falls in the day is measured at night, and the record, over the years, becomes a history of the rain of the place, and the history is used by farmers, and by water companies, and by engineers, and by anyone who needs to know how much rain falls, and when. The observer knows that her reading, on any one night, is a small thing, but she also knows that a thousand small readings, kept over a hundred years, are a climate, and that a climate is the most valuable thing a station can make.

The wind is measured by the anemometer, a set of cups that spin in the wind, and the faster they spin, the stronger the wind. The wind vane shows the direction, and the observer writes both down, and she also writes down the gusts, which are the sudden bursts that come and go, and which matter more than the steady wind, because it is the gust that does the damage, and that lifts the roof, and that breaks the branch. The observer has learned to feel the wind, as well as to measure it, and she can tell, from the sound of it in the trees, and the feel of it on her face, whether it is rising or falling, and whether it will be a quiet night or a wild one.

At midnight, the observer makes the second observation, and at three in the morning, the third. Between the observations, she keeps the log, and she checks the instruments, and she listens to the radio, and she reads, and she waits. The waiting is the hard part of the night shift, because the station is quiet, and the hill is dark, and the hours are long, and the observer must stay awake, and must stay ready, because the weather does not keep office hours, and a storm can come at any hour, and the observer must be there to see it, and to record it, and to warn the town below.

The storm, when it comes, is the test of the night shift, and the observer does not pretend otherwise. The wind rises, and the rain drives against the windows, and the anemometer spins faster and faster, and the barometer falls, and the observer goes out, in the rain, with her torch, to read the instruments, because the instruments must be read, even in a storm, and especially in a storm, because the storm is the weather that matters most. She writes the readings in the log, with wet hands, in a hand that is less neat than usual, and she writes down the gusts, and the direction, and the rain, and she does not stop, because the record must not stop, and the storm is the reason the station exists.

The dawn observation is the last of the night, and it is the one the observer likes best. The sky lightens, and the town below wakes, and the birds begin, and the observer reads the instruments for the last time, and writes the readings in the log, and closes the book, and goes home, to sleep while the town is awake. The day observer will come, and will read the log, and will see what the night did, and will carry the record on, and the station will go on, day and night, year after year, keeping the weather, and keeping the record, and keeping the town informed.

The thermometer lives in a white box, a shelter with louvred sides, raised off the ground, and the shelter has a name: a Stevenson screen, after the engineer who designed it. The screen keeps the sun off the thermometer and lets the air through, so that the temperature it measures is the temperature of the air, and not the temperature of the sun on the glass. The observer reads the thermometer through the slats, with her torch, and she reads it twice, to be sure, because a degree matters, and a degree misread is a degree lost from the record. The shelter is white because white reflects the sun, and it is on legs because the ground is warmer than the air, and every detail of it is a lesson in how to measure a thing without disturbing it.

Fog is the observer's hardest night, and it is the night when the station earns its keep. On a foggy night, the town below disappears, and the hill is an island in a white sea, and the observer can see no further than the fence of the enclosure. She writes down the visibility in metres, judged by which of the station's own landmarks she can still see: the mast, the fence, the shelter, the gate. The fog is a weather that must be measured by what it hides, and the observer, who knows the hill by heart, can tell, from the hiding, how thick the fog is, and whether it is lifting or settling. The fog will lift by morning, or it will not, and the observer's record will say which, and the town, when it wakes, will know whether to expect a clear day or a white one.

The observer was trained for this work, and the training was a long one, because the work looks simple and is not. She learned the instruments, and the log, and the codes, and the sky, and she learned them by doing, night after night, beside an observer who had done it for twenty years, until the routine became her own. She learned that a cloud is not only a cloud, but a kind, and a height, and a direction, and a speed, and that the kinds have names, and the names matter, because a cloud of one kind means rain, and a cloud of another means fair weather, and the difference is the difference between a forecast that is right and a forecast that is wrong.

The station's reports go out by radio, and by wire, to the forecast office, and to the town, and to anyone who needs to know the weather of the night. The observer sends the readings at the appointed hours, in a code that is short and exact, and the forecasters, who have the readings of a hundred stations, put them together into a map, and the map becomes the forecast, and the forecast becomes the warning, if a warning is needed. The observer never sees the forecast. She sees only the sky, and the instruments, and the log, and she trusts that her small part of it, kept faithfully, is enough.

The seasons change the night shift, as they change everything. In summer, the nights are short, and the observations are close together, and the sky is clear, and the work is light. In winter, the nights are long, and the observations are far apart, and the sky is often cloudy, and the work is heavy, with frost on the instruments, and snow to measure, and ice to clear from the rain gauge. The snow is measured with a ruler, on a board, in the enclosure, and the depth is written down, and the water in the melted snow is measured too, because a foot of snow is not a foot of water, and the water is what matters. The observer has measured snow in the dark, with a torch, in a wind that cuts through her coat, and she has written the depth in the log, with cold hands, and she has done it because the record must not stop, and the snow is the weather, and the weather is the work.

Lightning is the night the observer stays inside, and watches from the window, and writes down what she sees. The lightning is counted, and the thunder is timed, and the time between them tells the distance, because the light travels faster than the sound, and the observer, who knows this, can tell how far away the storm is, and whether it is coming closer or going away. The lightning is the one weather that cannot be measured by going out to it, and the observer, who has learned to respect it, measures it from safety, and writes it down, and waits for the storm to pass.

And on the calm nights, the nights when the sky is clear and the wind is still and the barometer is steady, the observer does the quietest part of the work, which is to look at the stars, and to write down that the night was fair, and the visibility was good, and the temperature fell slowly, and the dew settled on the grass. These nights are the ones that fill the log with the ordinary weather, and the ordinary weather is the most important weather of all, because it is the weather that most nights are, and the record of it, kept over a hundred years, is the climate of the place, and the climate is the story of the sky above the town, told one night at a time.

The record is the point of the station, and the record is what the night observer is keeping, one reading at a time, in the dark, on a hill, with a torch, and a logbook, and a knowledge of the sky that no machine can replace. The machines can measure the temperature, and the rain, and the wind, and they do, and they do it well. But the machines cannot look at the sky, and cannot judge the cloud, and cannot say whether the rain will come before dawn, or after it, or not at all. The human observer can, and does, and will, for as long as there are stations on hills, and nights to watch, and weather to record.

The night shift ends, and the observer goes home, and the sun comes up over the town, and the weather of the night becomes the weather of the day, and the log, with its columns of numbers and its plain descriptions of the sky, becomes a part of the history of the place. It is a small history, written one night at a time, by one person, in the dark, on a hill. And it is enough.

Demo program

# Demo: The Night Shift at the Weather Station
TEXT = "The Night Shift at the Weather Station\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nThe weather station in this article is an illustrative one, drawn from the ordinary practices of weather stations in many places. No single real station is described, and no real observer is named.\n\nThe night shift begins at ten o'clock, when the day observer goes home and the night observer takes the keys. The station is a small building on a hill, with a fenced enclosure of instruments outside, and a view, on a clear night, of the town below, with its lights, and of the sky above, with its stars. The night observer is a woman of forty, who has worked the night shift for six years, and who likes it, not because she dislikes people, but because the night is when the weather shows itself most plainly, and because the station, at night, belongs to her.\n\nThe first duty is the ten o'clock observation, and it is done the same way it has been done for a hundred years. The observer goes out to the enclosure, with a torch, and reads the instruments: the thermometer in its white shelter, the rain gauge, the wind vane and the anemometer on their mast, the barometer in the room, and the hygrometer that measures the dampness of the air. She writes the readings in the log, in the same columns, in the same order, every night, because the log is a record that must be comparable, night to night, year to year, and a record is only comparable if it is kept the same way. The readings take ten minutes, and they are the foundation of everything the station does.\n\nThen there is the sky. The observer looks up, and writes down the cloud: how much of the sky it covers, what kind it is, which way it is moving, and how fast. On a clear night, she writes down the stars, and the moon, and the visibility, which is how far she can see, judged by the lights of the town and the hills beyond it. On a cloudy night, she writes down the cloud base, judged by the way the cloud hides the hilltop, or does not. The sky is the part of the observation that cannot be read from an instrument, and it is the part that needs a human eye, and a human judgement, and a human memory of what the sky looked like last night, and the night before.\n\nThe barometer is the instrument the observer watches most closely, because it is the one that tells the future. The barometer measures the pressure of the air, and the pressure is falling, or rising, or steady, and the falling or rising tells the observer what the weather is doing, and what it will do next. A falling barometer means the weather is coming in, and a rising barometer means it is going out, and a barometer that falls fast means a storm, and a barometer that rises fast means a clearing. The observer has learned to read the barometer the way a sailor reads the sea, and she can tell, from the way it has moved over the last three hours, whether the rain will come before dawn, or after it, or not at all.\n\nThe rain gauge is a simple thing, a cylinder with a funnel, and it is read by pouring the water into a measure, and writing down the depth. The rain that falls at night is measured in the morning, and the rain that falls in the day is measured at night, and the record, over the years, becomes a history of the rain of the place, and the history is used by farmers, and by water companies, and by engineers, and by anyone who needs to know how much rain falls, and when. The observer knows that her reading, on any one night, is a small thing, but she also knows that a thousand small readings, kept over a hundred years, are a climate, and that a climate is the most valuable thing a station can make.\n\nThe wind is measured by the anemometer, a set of cups that spin in the wind, and the faster they spin, the stronger the wind. The wind vane shows the direction, and the observer writes both down, and she also writes down the gusts, which are the sudden bursts that come and go, and which matter more than the steady wind, because it is the gust that does the damage, and that lifts the roof, and that breaks the branch. The observer has learned to feel the wind, as well as to measure it, and she can tell, from the sound of it in the trees, and the feel of it on her face, whether it is rising or falling, and whether it will be a quiet night or a wild one.\n\nAt midnight, the observer makes the second observation, and at three in the morning, the third. Between the observations, she keeps the log, and she checks the instruments, and she listens to the radio, and she reads, and she waits. The waiting is the hard part of the night shift, because the station is quiet, and the hill is dark, and the hours are long, and the observer must stay awake, and must stay ready, because the weather does not keep office hours, and a storm can come at any hour, and the observer must be there to see it, and to record it, and to warn the town below.\n\nThe storm, when it comes, is the test of the night shift, and the observer does not pretend otherwise. The wind rises, and the rain drives against the windows, and the anemometer spins faster and faster, and the barometer falls, and the observer goes out, in the rain, with her torch, to read the instruments, because the instruments must be read, even in a storm, and especially in a storm, because the storm is the weather that matters most. She writes the readings in the log, with wet hands, in a hand that is less neat than usual, and she writes down the gusts, and the direction, and the rain, and she does not stop, because the record must not stop, and the storm is the reason the station exists.\n\nThe dawn observation is the last of the night, and it is the one the observer likes best. The sky lightens, and the town below wakes, and the birds begin, and the observer reads the instruments for the last time, and writes the readings in the log, and closes the book, and goes home, to sleep while the town is awake. The day observer will come, and will read the log, and will see what the night did, and will carry the record on, and the station will go on, day and night, year after year, keeping the weather, and keeping the record, and keeping the town informed.\n\nThe thermometer lives in a white box, a shelter with louvred sides, raised off the ground, and the shelter has a name: a Stevenson screen, after the engineer who designed it. The screen keeps the sun off the thermometer and lets the air through, so that the temperature it measures is the temperature of the air, and not the temperature of the sun on the glass. The observer reads the thermometer through the slats, with her torch, and she reads it twice, to be sure, because a degree matters, and a degree misread is a degree lost from the record. The shelter is white because white reflects the sun, and it is on legs because the ground is warmer than the air, and every detail of it is a lesson in how to measure a thing without disturbing it.\n\nFog is the observer's hardest night, and it is the night when the station earns its keep. On a foggy night, the town below disappears, and the hill is an island in a white sea, and the observer can see no further than the fence of the enclosure. She writes down the visibility in metres, judged by which of the station's own landmarks she can still see: the mast, the fence, the shelter, the gate. The fog is a weather that must be measured by what it hides, and the observer, who knows the hill by heart, can tell, from the hiding, how thick the fog is, and whether it is lifting or settling. The fog will lift by morning, or it will not, and the observer's record will say which, and the town, when it wakes, will know whether to expect a clear day or a white one.\n\nThe observer was trained for this work, and the training was a long one, because the work looks simple and is not. She learned the instruments, and the log, and the codes, and the sky, and she learned them by doing, night after night, beside an observer who had done it for twenty years, until the routine became her own. She learned that a cloud is not only a cloud, but a kind, and a height, and a direction, and a speed, and that the kinds have names, and the names matter, because a cloud of one kind means rain, and a cloud of another means fair weather, and the difference is the difference between a forecast that is right and a forecast that is wrong.\n\nThe station's reports go out by radio, and by wire, to the forecast office, and to the town, and to anyone who needs to know the weather of the night. The observer sends the readings at the appointed hours, in a code that is short and exact, and the forecasters, who have the readings of a hundred stations, put them together into a map, and the map becomes the forecast, and the forecast becomes the warning, if a warning is needed. The observer never sees the forecast. She sees only the sky, and the instruments, and the log, and she trusts that her small part of it, kept faithfully, is enough.\n\nThe seasons change the night shift, as they change everything. In summer, the nights are short, and the observations are close together, and the sky is clear, and the work is light. In winter, the nights are long, and the observations are far apart, and the sky is often cloudy, and the work is heavy, with frost on the instruments, and snow to measure, and ice to clear from the rain gauge. The snow is measured with a ruler, on a board, in the enclosure, and the depth is written down, and the water in the melted snow is measured too, because a foot of snow is not a foot of water, and the water is what matters. The observer has measured snow in the dark, with a torch, in a wind that cuts through her coat, and she has written the depth in the log, with cold hands, and she has done it because the record must not stop, and the snow is the weather, and the weather is the work.\n\nLightning is the night the observer stays inside, and watches from the window, and writes down what she sees. The lightning is counted, and the thunder is timed, and the time between them tells the distance, because the light travels faster than the sound, and the observer, who knows this, can tell how far away the storm is, and whether it is coming closer or going away. The lightning is the one weather that cannot be measured by going out to it, and the observer, who has learned to respect it, measures it from safety, and writes it down, and waits for the storm to pass.\n\nAnd on the calm nights, the nights when the sky is clear and the wind is still and the barometer is steady, the observer does the quietest part of the work, which is to look at the stars, and to write down that the night was fair, and the visibility was good, and the temperature fell slowly, and the dew settled on the grass. These nights are the ones that fill the log with the ordinary weather, and the ordinary weather is the most important weather of all, because it is the weather that most nights are, and the record of it, kept over a hundred years, is the climate of the place, and the climate is the story of the sky above the town, told one night at a time.\n\nThe record is the point of the station, and the record is what the night observer is keeping, one reading at a time, in the dark, on a hill, with a torch, and a logbook, and a knowledge of the sky that no machine can replace. The machines can measure the temperature, and the rain, and the wind, and they do, and they do it well. But the machines cannot look at the sky, and cannot judge the cloud, and cannot say whether the rain will come before dawn, or after it, or not at all. The human observer can, and does, and will, for as long as there are stations on hills, and nights to watch, and weather to record.\n\nThe night shift ends, and the observer goes home, and the sun comes up over the town, and the weather of the night becomes the weather of the day, and the log, with its columns of numbers and its plain descriptions of the sky, becomes a part of the history of the place. It is a small history, written one night at a time, by one person, in the dark, on a hill. And it is enough.\n"

A how-it-works explainer on how a hand pump lifts water, by removing air and letting the weight of the atmosphere do the lifting.

How a Hand Pump Lifts Water By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

A hand pump is a humble machine, and it is one of the most important machines in the world. It stands beside a well, or over a borehole, in a yard, or a field, or a village square, and it does a simple thing: it lifts water from below the ground to above it, using nothing but the strength of a human arm. There is no engine, no electricity, no fuel. There is a handle, and a rod, and a pipe, and a valve, and the cleverness of a principle that is more than two thousand years old. How it does it is a story about air, and pressure, and the weight of the world.

Water underground does not sit in a lake. It sits in the ground itself, in the spaces between grains of sand and gravel, in cracks in the rock, in a layer called the water table, below which the ground is soaked and above which it is dry. A well is a hole dug down to that layer, and the water seeps into the hole, and stands in it at the level of the water table. A hand pump does not reach down and grab the water. It does something subtler. It removes the air from the pipe, and lets the weight of the air above do the lifting.

The air has weight. This is the first thing to understand, and it is the strangest, because we do not feel it. The air above us, from the ground to the top of the sky, presses down on everything, with a weight of about fourteen pounds on every square inch. We do not feel it because the air presses on us from all sides at once, and because the air inside our bodies presses back with the same force. But the weight is there, and it is the weight that lifts the water in a pump. When the air is removed from the top of the pipe, there is nothing pressing down on the water inside the pipe, and the air pressing down on the water outside the pipe, in the well, pushes the water up the pipe, to fill the empty space. The pump does not lift the water. The atmosphere lifts it, and the pump merely makes a space for it to be lifted into.

The pump makes that space with a piston, a tight-fitting plug that slides up and down inside a cylinder, at the bottom of the pipe or at the top of it, according to the design. The piston has a valve in it, a small flap or ball that lets water pass upward through it but not downward. At the bottom of the pipe there is another valve, the foot valve, which lets water come up into the pipe but not go back down into the well. The two valves, working together, are the heart of the pump, and they are what turn the up-and-down movement of the handle into a steady stream of water.

Here is how it works, stroke by stroke. The handle is pushed down, and the rod pulls the piston up. As the piston rises, it leaves an empty space below it, and the air in that space is thin, and the water below, pushed by the atmosphere, rises through the foot valve to fill it. The foot valve closes behind the water, holding it in the pipe. Then the handle is pulled up, and the rod pushes the piston down. The piston presses on the water below it, and the water, having nowhere else to go, pushes open the valve in the piston itself, and flows up through the piston, to the space above it. The piston rises again, and this time it lifts the water above it, and the water above the piston is pushed out of the spout, and more water rises from below to take its place. Stroke by stroke, the water climbs, a pistonful at a time, until it flows from the spout in a steady stream.

The first strokes are the hardest, because the pipe is full of air, and the air must be pumped out before the water can rise. The pump wheezes and gurgles, and nothing comes out, and the person at the handle wonders whether the pump is broken. It is not broken. It is priming, and priming takes time, and the time it takes depends on how deep the water is, and how tight the piston is, and how patient the pumper is. Once the water reaches the piston, the work becomes easier, and the water flows, and the pump settles into its rhythm, and the rhythm is the sound of water being lifted by air.

There is a limit to how high a pump of this kind can lift water, and the limit is set by the weight of the air itself. The atmosphere can support a column of water about thirty-four feet high, and no more, because a column taller than that would weigh more than the air pressing on it, and the air could not hold it up. A pump that lifts water by suction, with its piston at the top, cannot lift water from deeper than about twenty-five feet in practice, because the seal is never perfect, and the air leaks, and the water falls back. For deeper wells, the piston must go down to the bottom, to the water itself, and push the water up, instead of pulling it. The deep-well pump has its cylinder at the bottom of the borehole, and the rod runs all the way down to it, and the handle at the top works the piston far below, and the water is pushed up the pipe, foot by foot, to the spout.

The handle is a lever, and the lever is what makes the work possible for a human arm. The handle is long, and the rod is attached close to the pivot, so that a long, easy movement of the hand becomes a short, strong movement of the rod. The person at the handle does not need to be strong. They need to be steady, and to keep the rhythm, and to let the lever do the work. A child can work a hand pump, and an old person can work one, and a village can depend on one, because the machine asks for effort, but not for strength, and it asks for it in a rhythm that a human body can keep for as long as the water is needed.

The pump needs care, and the care is simple, and it is the kind of care that a community can give. The leather seal on the piston wears out, and it must be replaced, and the replacement is a piece of leather, cut to size, soaked in oil, and fitted by hand. The valves wear, and they must be cleaned, and the grit that comes up with the water must be cleared, and the rod must be oiled, and the handle must be tightened, and the spout must be kept clear. None of this needs a specialist. It needs a person who knows the pump, and who checks it, and who fixes it before it breaks, and every village with a hand pump has such a person, whether they know it or not, and the pump depends on them.

The pump breaks, of course, and when it breaks, the village knows it at once, because the water stops, and the water is life. A broken pump is not a small thing. It is a day without water for drinking, and for cooking, and for washing, and for the animals, and the day becomes a walk to the next well, or to the river, carrying water in jars, the way people did before the pump was there. The pump is repaired quickly, because it must be, and the repair is done by the person who knows it, with the spare seal, and the spare valve, and the tools that are kept beside it, and the water flows again, and the village goes back to its business, and the pump goes back to its work, which is the work of lifting water, stroke by stroke, day after day.

There are newer pumps, and there are better pumps, and there are pumps that run on electricity, and on the sun, and on the wind, and they lift more water, and they lift it faster, and they lift it from deeper wells. But the hand pump remains, and it remains for a reason: it needs nothing but a human arm, and a little care, and it works when the power is out, and when the fuel has run out, and when the sun has gone down, and it works for the person who needs water now, in the yard, at the well, with a handle, and a rod, and a pipe, and a valve, and the weight of the air.

The well itself is older than the pump, and it deserves a word, because the pump is only as good as the well it stands over. A well is dug by hand, or bored by machine, down through the soil and the rock, until it reaches the water, and it is lined, with brick, or concrete, or stone, so that the sides do not fall in, and it is covered, so that nothing falls into it, and the water in it is the water of the ground, filtered by the earth it has passed through. The well-digger is a skilled worker, and a brave one, working in a hole in the ground, in the dark, with the earth above him, and the water below, and the well he digs may serve a village for a hundred years, if it is dug well, and kept clean, and not overdrawn.

Winter is the pump's enemy, and the pump must be protected from it. Water left in the pipe will freeze, and the ice will expand, and the pipe will split, and the pump will be useless until the spring. The pump's keeper drains the pipe in the coldest weather, or wraps it, or keeps it working, because a pump that is worked does not freeze, and a pump that stands idle does. The cold is a test of the pump, and of the keeper, and of the village, and the village that keeps its pump working through the winter is a village that has learned to look after what it depends on.

The pump is also a social place, and it is worth saying so, because a machine that gives water becomes a meeting place, whether it wants to be or not. The queue at the pump, in the morning, is where the news of the village is exchanged, and where the children play, and where the old sit and talk, and where a stranger is noticed, and welcomed, or not. The pump is the village's tap, and its noticeboard, and its square, all in one, and the time spent waiting for water is not wasted time. It is the time when the village is a village, gathered around the thing it needs most.

The sound of the pump is the sound of water, and it is a sound that a village knows by heart. The creak of the handle, the clunk of the piston, the gurgle of the water rising, the splash of the spout: these are the sounds of the morning, and of the evening, and they mean that the water is there, and that the day can go on. A village without the sound of its pump is a village that is holding its breath, and a village with it is a village that is alive, and working, and the sound is as much a part of the place as the church bell, or the market, or the birds.

And the repair kit is the pump's insurance, and it is kept in a box beside the pump, or in the house of the person who knows the pump best. The box holds a spare leather seal, and a spare valve, and a spanner, and a piece of wire, and a length of rope, and the knowledge of how to use them. The kit is small, and it is cheap, and it is the difference between a pump that is repaired in an hour and a pump that is broken for a week. Every pump needs a kit, and every village needs a person who knows where the kit is, and how to use it, and the pump, and the kit, and the person, together, are what keep the water flowing.

The hand pump is a lesson in how much can be done with how little. It does not fight the world. It uses the world: the weight of the air, the pressure of the atmosphere, the lever of the handle, the seal of the piston, the patience of the person at the handle. It lifts water not by force but by making a space, and letting the world fill it. That is a good way to lift water, and it is a good way to do almost anything: make the space, and let the world do the work, and keep the rhythm, and the water will come.

Demo program

# Demo: How a Hand Pump Lifts Water
TEXT = "How a Hand Pump Lifts Water\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nA hand pump is a humble machine, and it is one of the most important machines in the world. It stands beside a well, or over a borehole, in a yard, or a field, or a village square, and it does a simple thing: it lifts water from below the ground to above it, using nothing but the strength of a human arm. There is no engine, no electricity, no fuel. There is a handle, and a rod, and a pipe, and a valve, and the cleverness of a principle that is more than two thousand years old. How it does it is a story about air, and pressure, and the weight of the world.\n\nWater underground does not sit in a lake. It sits in the ground itself, in the spaces between grains of sand and gravel, in cracks in the rock, in a layer called the water table, below which the ground is soaked and above which it is dry. A well is a hole dug down to that layer, and the water seeps into the hole, and stands in it at the level of the water table. A hand pump does not reach down and grab the water. It does something subtler. It removes the air from the pipe, and lets the weight of the air above do the lifting.\n\nThe air has weight. This is the first thing to understand, and it is the strangest, because we do not feel it. The air above us, from the ground to the top of the sky, presses down on everything, with a weight of about fourteen pounds on every square inch. We do not feel it because the air presses on us from all sides at once, and because the air inside our bodies presses back with the same force. But the weight is there, and it is the weight that lifts the water in a pump. When the air is removed from the top of the pipe, there is nothing pressing down on the water inside the pipe, and the air pressing down on the water outside the pipe, in the well, pushes the water up the pipe, to fill the empty space. The pump does not lift the water. print(f"Article: How a Hand Pump Lifts Water")

reading_minutes = math.ceil(words / 200)
the_count = len(re.findall(r'\bthe\b', TEXT, re.IGNORECASE))

Full text

A Review of the Idea of the Public Library By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

This review is of an idea, not of a book. The public library is an idea before it is a building, and the idea is this: that the knowledge of the world, and the stories of the world, should be available to everyone, for free, in a place that belongs to everyone. The review that follows asks whether the idea works, what it costs, what it gives, and whether it is worth keeping.

The idea is old, and it has been tried in many forms. In the ancient world, libraries belonged to kings and to temples, and they were open to scholars, and to priests, and to the few who could read. The public library, the library that belongs to the public and is open to the public, is a newer thing, and it grew out of a simple conviction: that a town is better when its people can read, and that people will read if books are put within their reach, and that the cost of putting them there is a cost worth paying. The first public libraries were paid for by gifts, and by subscriptions, and by the rates, and they were opened with pride, and they were used at once, by people who had never owned a book and never expected to.

The library, as a building, is a plain thing, and its plainness is part of its virtue. It is a room, or several rooms, full of shelves, and the shelves are full of books, and the books are arranged so that a person can find what they want, and can find, beside it, something they did not know they wanted. There is a desk, and a person at the desk, who will help, and who will answer a question, and who will order a book that is not there, and who will keep the place quiet enough to read in. There is a table, and chairs, and light, and heat, and a silence that is not the silence of emptiness but the silence of many people reading at once. The library is not a grand building. It is a useful one, and its usefulness is its grandeur.

The first thing the library gives is books, and it gives them without a price. A person who cannot afford to buy a book can borrow one, and can read it, and can bring it back, and can borrow another. The library does not ask for money, and it does not ask for proof of worthiness, and it does not ask what the book is for. It asks only that the book be brought back, so that someone else can borrow it. This is a radical idea, and it has survived for two centuries, because it works. The library is the one place in a town where a poor person and a rich person are treated exactly the same, and where the poor person has the same access to the same books, and the same right to sit and read them.

The second thing the library gives is a place. A town needs places where a person can go without buying anything, and without being asked to leave, and the library is the best of them. It is warm in winter, and cool in summer, and it is open to the old, and to the young, and to the lonely, and to the stranger, and it asks nothing of them but that they behave. The library is a refuge, and it is a refuge that does not advertise itself as one, which is why it works. A person who has nowhere to go can go to the library, and can sit, and can read, and can be among people, and can be left alone, and that is a thing that a town needs, and that few towns provide.

The third thing the library gives is help, and the help is human. The librarian is not a guard, and not a clerk, but a guide, and the guide's work is to know the books, and to know the people, and to put the two together. A child who asks for a story is given a story, and then another, and then a book that is a little harder, and a little better. An adult who asks for a book on a subject is given the book, and the book beside it, and the book that argues the opposite. A person who asks how to do something, how to fill in a form, how to find a job, how to learn a language, is helped, patiently, and without judgement. The librarian's work is invisible, and it is the library's greatest asset, because a library without a librarian is a warehouse, and a library with one is a place of learning.

The library has its faults, and they should be named honestly. The books are not all good, and some of them are out of date, and some of them are never borrowed, and the shelves are full of things that nobody wants. The building is often tired, and the heating is often broken, and the opening hours are often too short, and the budget is often too small. The library is, in many towns, the first thing to be cut when money is short, and the last thing to be restored when money returns, because its benefits are slow, and quiet, and hard to measure, and the people who use it most are the people with the least voice. These are real faults, and they are faults of neglect, not of the idea.

The idea has been challenged, in recent years, by the claim that the library is no longer needed, because the books are on the screen, and the screen is everywhere. The claim is wrong, and it is wrong in a way that matters. The screen gives information, and it gives it fast, and it gives it in abundance. But the screen does not give a place, and it does not give a librarian, and it does not give the silence of a room full of readers, and it does not give a book that can be held, and read slowly, and returned, and borrowed again. The screen is a wonderful thing, and the library has learned to use it, and to offer it, and to teach people to use it. But the screen is not a library, and a town that replaces its library with a screen has lost something that the screen cannot give back.

The library has also been challenged by the claim that it costs too much, and that the money would be better spent elsewhere. The library is not cheap, and it should not pretend to be. It costs money to buy books, and to keep them, and to pay librarians, and to heat the building, and to keep the doors open. But the cost is small, measured against what it gives, and against what it would cost to do without it. A town without a library is a town where the poor cannot read for free, where the lonely have nowhere to go, where the children have nowhere to do their homework, and where the knowledge of the world is available only to those who can pay for it. The library is not an expense. It is an investment, and it is one of the cheapest investments a town can make, because it pays in reading, and in learning, and in the quiet dignity of a place that belongs to everyone.

The children's section is the library's future, and it is the part of the building that is loudest, and happiest, and most important. The children's shelves are low, so that a small person can reach them, and the books are bright, and the chairs are small, and the room is full of the noise of children choosing, and arguing, and reading aloud, and asking for one more story. A child who learns to love the library will love it for life, and a child who is read to in the library, on a librarian's knee, or on a parent's, has learned the most important lesson the library can teach: that books are a pleasure, and that the pleasure is free, and that it belongs to them. The children's section is not a corner of the library. It is the library's reason for being.

The library has also learned to live with the internet, and the learning has been a good one. The library offers computers, and it offers them for free, to people who have no computer of their own, and it teaches people to use them, patiently, and without condescension, and it helps people to fill in forms, and to apply for jobs, and to write letters, and to learn. The library is, for many people, the only place where the internet is free, and where help with it is free, and where nobody is in a hurry to get you off the machine. The screen has not replaced the library. It has given the library a new job, and the library has taken it up, as it has taken up every new job in its history, and done it well.

The library is also a place of refuge in a crisis, and it is worth naming this, because it is a role that is rarely planned and often needed. In a heatwave, the library is cool, and its doors are open, and the old, and the sick, and the homeless, can sit in it, and be safe. In a cold snap, the library is warm, and its doors are open, and the same people can sit in it, and be safe. In a time of trouble, the library is a place where information is available, and where help is available, and where a person can sit, and think, and plan, and the library asks nothing in return. A town that has a library has a place to go when things go wrong, and a town that does not has one place fewer.

The library's book sale is a small thing, and it is worth a mention, because it shows the library's economy at its most human. The books that are worn out, or out of date, or duplicated, are sold, for a small sum, on a table by the door, and the money goes back into the library, and the books go to new homes, and the people who buy them are the people who love books, and who cannot afford new ones, and who are glad of the library's cast-offs. The book sale is not a fundraiser of any great size. It is a circulation, a way of keeping books moving, and of keeping the library in touch with the people who use it, and it is one of the library's quiet pleasures.

The volunteers are the library's other workforce, and they deserve a word, because they do the work that the budget cannot pay for. They shelve the books, and they read to the children, and they run the book groups, and they keep the garden, and they do it for nothing, because they love the library, and because they believe in it. A library with volunteers is a library with friends, and a library with friends is a library that will survive, because its friends will defend it, and will speak for it, and will keep its doors open, when the money is short and the future is uncertain.

The future of the library is often predicted, and the predictions are usually wrong. The library has been declared dead a dozen times, by the coming of the radio, and of the television, and of the internet, and of the electronic book, and it has survived them all, because it is not a technology. It is an idea, and the idea is that knowledge should be free, and available, and shared, and that a town should have a place where that happens. The future library will have fewer books, perhaps, and more computers, and more rooms for meeting, and more help for the people who need it. But it will still be a library, and it will still be free, and it will still belong to everyone, and it will still be worth defending.

The idea of the public library deserves five stars, and it deserves them not because it is perfect, but because it is the best idea of its kind that has ever been tried, and because it has been tried, in thousands of towns, for two hundred years, and because it works. The faults are real, and they are faults of funding, and of neglect, and they can be fixed, by funding, and by attention, and by the simple decision to keep the doors open, and the shelves full, and the librarians paid. The idea does not need to be reinvented. It needs to be kept, and to be used, and to be defended, by the people who use it, and by the people who pay for it, and by the people who understand what it is.

Go to your library. Borrow a book. Sit and read it. Ask the librarian a question. Take a child, and let the child choose a book, and watch the child's face. The library is there, and it is free, and it is yours, and it has been yours for two hundred years, and it will be yours for two hundred more, if you use it, and if you defend it, and if you remember what it is: a room full of books, in a town, open to everyone, for free. That is the idea, and it is a good one, and it is worth five stars, and worth keeping.

The books are not all good, and some of them are out of date, and some of them are never borrowed, and the shelves are full of things that nobody wants. The building is often tired, and the heating is often broken, and the opening hours are often too short, and the budget is often too small. The library is, in many towns, the first thing to be cut when money is short, and the last thing to be restored when money returns, because its benefits are slow, and quiet, and hard to measure, and the people who use it most are the people with the least voice. These are real faults, and they are faults of neglect, not of the idea.\n\nThe idea has been challenged, in recent years, by the claim that the library is no longer needed, because the books are on the screen, and the screen is everywhere. The claim is wrong, and it is wrong in a way that matters. The screen gives information, and it gives it fast, and it gives it in abundance. But the screen does not give a place, and it does not give a librarian, and it does not give the silence of a room full of readers, and it does not give a book that can be held, and read slowly, and returned, and borrowed again. The screen is a wonderful thing, and the library has learned to use it, and to offer it, and to teach people to use it. But the screen is not a library, and a town that replaces its library with a screen has lost something that the screen cannot give back.\n\nThe library has also been challenged by the claim that it costs too much, and that the money would be better spent elsewhere. The library is not cheap, and it should not pretend to be. It costs money to buy books, and to keep them, and to pay librarians, and to heat the building, and to keep the doors open. But the cost is small, measured against what it gives, and against what it would cost to do without it. A town without a library is a town where the poor cannot read for free, where the lonely have nowhere to go, where the children have nowhere to do their homework, and where the knowledge of the world is available only to those who can pay for it. The library is not an expense. It is an investment, and it is one of the cheapest investments a town can make, because it pays in reading, and in learning, and in the quiet dignity of a place that belongs to everyone.\n\nThe children's section is the library's future, and it is the part of the building that is loudest, and happiest, and most important. The children's shelves are low, so that a small person can reach them, and the books are bright, and the chairs are small, and the room is full of the noise of children choosing, and arguing, and reading aloud, and asking for one more story. A child who learns to love the library will love it for life, and a child who is read to in the library, on a librarian's knee, or on a parent's, has learned the most important lesson the library can teach: that books are a pleasure, and that the pleasure is free, and that it belongs to them. The children's section is not a corner of the library. It is the library's reason for being.\n\nThe library has also learned to live with the internet, and the learning has been a good one. The library offers computers, and it offers them for free, to people who have no computer of their own, and it teaches people to use them, patiently, and without condescension, and it helps people to fill in forms, and to apply for jobs, and to write letters, and to learn. The library is, for many people, the only place where the internet is free, and where help with it is free, and where nobody is in a hurry to get you off the machine. The screen has not replaced the library. It has given the library a new job, and the library has taken it up, as it has taken up every new job in its history, and done it well.\n\nThe library is also a place of refuge in a crisis, and it is worth naming this, because it is a role that is rarely planned and often needed. In a heatwave, the library is cool, and its doors are open, and the old, and the sick, and the homeless, can sit in it, and be safe. In a cold snap, the library is warm, and its doors are open, and the same people can sit in it, and be safe. In a time of trouble, the library is a place where information is available, and where help is available, and where a person can sit, and think, and plan, and the library asks nothing in return. A town that has a library has a place to go when things go wrong, and a town that does not has one place fewer.\n\nThe library's book sale is a small thing, and it is worth a mention, because it shows the library's economy at its most human. The books that are worn out, or out of date, or duplicated, are sold, for a small sum, on a table by the door, and the money goes back into the library, and the books go to new homes, and the people who buy them are the people who love books, and who cannot afford new ones, and who are glad of the library's cast-offs. The book sale is not a fundraiser of any great size. It is a circulation, a way of keeping books moving, and of keeping the library in touch with the people who use it, and it is one of the library's quiet pleasures.\n\nThe volunteers are the library's other workforce, and they deserve a word, because they do the work that the budget cannot pay for. They shelve the books, and they read to the children, and they run the book groups, and they keep the garden, and they do it for nothing, because they love the library, and because they believe in it. A library with volunteers is a library with friends, and a library with friends is a library that will survive, because its friends will defend it, and will speak for it, and will keep its doors open, when the money is short and the future is uncertain.\n\nThe future of the library is often predicted, and the predictions are usually wrong. The library has been declared dead a dozen times, by the coming of the radio, and of the television, and of the internet, and of the electronic book, and it has survived them all, because it is not a technology. It is an idea, and the idea is that knowledge should be free, and available, and shared, and that a town should have a place where that happens. The future library will have fewer books, perhaps, and more computers, and more rooms for meeting, and more help for the people who need it. But it will still be a library, and it will still be free, and it will still belong to everyone, and it will still be worth defending.\n\nThe idea of the public library deserves five stars, and it deserves them not because it is perfect, but because it is the best idea of its kind that has ever been tried, and because it has been tried, in thousands of towns, for two hundred years, and because it works. The faults are real, and they are faults of funding, and of neglect, and they can be fixed, by funding, and by attention, and by the simple decision to keep the doors open, and the shelves full, and the librarians paid. The idea does not need to be reinvented. It needs to be kept, and to be used, and to be defended, by the people who use it, and by the people who pay for it, and by the people who understand what it is.\n\nGo to your library. Borrow a book. Sit and read it. Ask the librarian a question. Take a child, and let the child choose a book, and watch the child's face. The library is there, and it is free, and it is yours, and it has been yours for two hundred years, and it will be yours for two hundred more, if you use it, and if you defend it, and if you remember what it is: a room full of books, in a town, open to everyone, for free. That is the idea, and it is a good one, and it is worth five stars, and worth keeping.\n"

Full text

A Short History of the Idea of Zero By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

Zero is the strangest number there is, and it is also the newest. We use it every day, without thinking, to count, and to measure, and to calculate, and we forget that for most of human history, nobody had it. The history of zero is the history of an idea that was resisted, and misunderstood, and feared, and that finally, after centuries, changed the world.

The first thing to understand is that zero is two ideas, not one, and the two ideas arrived at different times. The first is zero as a placeholder, a mark that says: there is nothing in this place. The second is zero as a number in its own right, a quantity that can be added, and subtracted, and multiplied, and that has a meaning of its own. The placeholder came first, and it came because it was needed. The number came later, and it came because it was true.

The need for a placeholder arose from the way numbers were written. The earliest systems of writing numbers used a mark for each unit, and a different mark for each ten, and each hundred, and so on, and they did not need a zero, because the absence of a mark was enough. But when numbers began to be written in columns, with each column worth ten times the one before it, a problem appeared. How do you write the number that is three hundreds, no tens, and five ones? You need a mark in the tens column, to show that the column is empty, and to keep the three and the five in their proper places. Without the mark, the number would read as thirty-five, and the hundreds would be lost. The mark was invented, in different places, in different forms, and it was a dot, or a space, or a small circle, and it said: nothing here, and the nothing matters.

The Babylonians, who wrote their numbers in clay, used a mark for the empty place, and they used it for centuries, but they did not think of it as a number. It was a punctuation, a way of keeping the columns straight, and nothing more. The Greeks, who were the great mathematicians of the ancient world, did not use it at all, and they did not need it, because their numbers were written with letters, and a letter could not be empty. The Greeks were suspicious of the idea of nothing, and their philosophy said that nothing could not be something, and that the void could not exist, and their mathematics, brilliant as it was, had no place for zero.

The idea travelled east, and it was in India that the placeholder became a number. The Indian mathematicians, working in the first centuries of the modern era, used a small circle for the empty place, and they called it by a word that meant empty, or void, and they began, slowly, to treat it as a number like any other. They wrote down the rules for it: that a number added to zero is unchanged, that a number multiplied by zero is zero, that zero subtracted from a number leaves the number unchanged. These rules seem obvious to us, and they were not obvious then, because they required a person to think of nothing as a thing, and to do arithmetic with it, and to trust the result.

The Indian zero travelled west, with the Indian numerals, through the Arab world, and into Europe, and it arrived in Europe in the Middle Ages, carried by merchants and by scholars, and it was resisted. The merchants saw its use at once, because it made calculation easier, and because it made bookkeeping possible on a scale that the old Roman numerals could not match. The scholars resisted it, because it came from outside, and because it was associated with a foreign religion, and because it seemed to say that nothing was something, which offended the philosophy they had inherited from the Greeks. There were towns that forbade its use, and schools that refused to teach it, and men who were called heretics for using it, and the resistance lasted for centuries.

But the merchants won, because the merchants had the need, and the need was practical. A merchant who could calculate with the new numerals could keep better books, and could trade further, and could grow richer, and the richness spread, and the numerals spread with it, and the zero spread with the numerals. By the end of the Middle Ages, the zero was in use all over Europe, and the old Roman numerals were kept only for the things that did not need calculation: the dates on buildings, and the chapters of books, and the faces of clocks. The zero had won, not by argument, but by use, and by the simple fact that it worked.

The zero made modern mathematics possible, and modern mathematics made the modern world possible. Without zero, there is no place-value system, and without a place-value system, there is no easy arithmetic, and without easy arithmetic, there is no algebra, and without algebra, there is no calculus, and without calculus, there is no physics, and no engineering, and no modern world. The zero is the foundation of all of it, and it is a foundation made of nothing, which is the strangest thing of all. The whole of modern science, and modern technology, and modern finance, rests on a mark that means nothing, and that is why the history of zero is worth telling.

There is one operation that zero refuses, and it is the operation that shows its strangeness most clearly. You cannot divide by zero. A number divided by zero has no answer, because there is no number that, multiplied by zero, gives the number you started with. The mathematicians tried, for centuries, to make it work, and they could not, and they learned, in the end, that the refusal was not a failure but a truth: that zero is a number, but it is a number with its own rules, and that its rules are not the rules of the other numbers. Division by zero is the place where arithmetic breaks down, and where the mathematician must stop, and admit that the nothing has a limit, and that the limit is part of its meaning.

The zero also changed the way we think about the world, and this is the part of its history that is hardest to see, because it is not written in numbers but in ideas. Before zero, the world was thought of as full, and as positive, and as countable, and the idea of nothing was a philosophical problem, and a theological one. After zero, the world could be thought of as containing emptiness, and the emptiness could be measured, and used, and the idea of nothing became a tool, and the tool became a science. The zero taught us that nothing is not the absence of thought, but a thought in itself, and that the void, which the Greeks feared, could be written down, and counted, and used.

Zero was not invented once. It was invented more than once, in more than one place, and the other invention is worth telling, because it shows that the idea was not an accident. In the Americas, the Maya, who built great cities and kept a calendar more accurate than Europe's, invented a zero of their own, a shell-shaped mark, used as a placeholder in their numbers, and used in their calendar, to mark the beginning of a cycle. The Maya zero and the Indian zero were invented independently, by peoples who never met, and who never heard of each other, and both of them invented it for the same reason: because a system of numbers that counts in columns needs a mark for the empty column, and because the mark, once invented, is too useful to give up.

The abacus, which is older than zero, shows the idea in another form. On an abacus, a column with no beads moved is a column that holds nothing, and the emptiness is visible, and it is meaningful, and the person using the abacus knows, without a mark, that the column is empty. The abacus is a machine for calculation, and it works without a written zero, because the zero is there in the machine, in the empty column, and in the hand that knows it is empty. The written zero is the abacus made into a mark, and the mark made into a number, and the number made into a science.

The man who brought the Indian numerals to Europe deserves a name, and his name is Fibonacci, a merchant's son from Pisa, who learned the numerals in North Africa, and who wrote a book about them, called the Book of Calculation, in the year 1202. The book showed, with worked examples, how the new numerals could be used for trade, and for bookkeeping, and for calculation, and it showed that a merchant who used them could do in minutes what a merchant using Roman numerals took hours to do. The book was copied, and taught, and argued over, and it changed the arithmetic of Europe, and it changed it because it was useful, and because usefulness, in the end, beats tradition.

Zero changed algebra, and algebra changed the world. An equation is a balance, and the zero is the point of balance, the place where the two sides are equal, and the place from which the unknown is found. To solve an equation is to find the value that makes it zero, and the zero, which began as a mark for an empty column, became the answer to the question, and the question itself. The algebra that grew from zero is the algebra of the modern world, and it is the algebra of the rocket, and of the bridge, and of the computer, and it all rests on the small circle that means nothing.

There is a curiosity in our calendar, and it is a curiosity that zero explains. There is no year zero. The calendar goes from the year 1 before the common era to the year 1 of the common era, with nothing between them, because the calendar was made before zero was known in Europe, and because the people who made it counted the years the way they counted everything: starting at one, not at zero. The astronomers, who need a year zero for their calculations, have added one, and they count the year before the common era as the year zero, and the year before that as minus one, and the calendar, which is a human invention, has been corrected by the zero, which is also a human invention, and the correction is a small thing, and it shows how deeply zero has entered our thinking.

Zero is at the heart of the computer, and the computer is at the heart of the modern world, and the connection is worth a paragraph, because it is the strangest of all. A computer works with two numbers only, zero and one, and it does everything it does by combining them, millions of times a second, in patterns that become words, and pictures, and music, and calculations. The zero in a computer is not a number. It is a state, the state of a switch that is off, and the one is the state of a switch that is on, and the whole of computing is built on the difference between on and off, between something and nothing. The zero, which was once a mark for an empty column, has become the off-switch of the world.

Zero and infinity are related, and the relation is a deep one, and it is the reason mathematicians have spent centuries thinking about both. Divide a number by a number that gets smaller and smaller, closer and closer to zero, and the answer gets larger and larger, closer and closer to infinity, and the zero and the infinity are two ends of the same idea: the smallest thing there is, and the largest. The zero is the beginning of counting, and infinity is the end of it, and the two of them, together, frame the whole of mathematics, and the whole of the world that mathematics describes.

The symbol itself, the small circle, is worth a last word, because it is the same in every language, and it is understood everywhere, and it needs no translation. A zero written in London is a zero written in Delhi, and a zero written in Delhi is a zero written in Beijing, and the circle is the same, and the meaning is the same, and the idea is the same. The zero is the most universal symbol ever invented, and it is a circle, and a circle has no beginning and no end, and it is empty in the middle, and it is the perfect shape for the idea it carries: nothing, and everything that nothing makes possible.

The history of zero is a short history, as histories go, and it is a history of an idea that was invented, and resisted, and adopted, and that changed everything. It is a reminder that the most powerful ideas are often the simplest, and that the simplest idea of all, the idea that nothing can be something, took the longest to accept, and did the most when it was accepted. The next time you write a zero, think of the centuries it took to get there, and of the merchants who used it in secret, and of the scholars who feared it, and of the mathematicians who trusted it, and of the world that was built on it, out of nothing, and out of a small circle, drawn in the sand, that said: there is nothing here, and the nothing matters.

Demo program

It is a state, the state of a switch that is off, and the one is the state of a switch that is on, and the whole of computing is built on the difference between on and off, between something and nothing. The zero, which was once a mark for an empty column, has become the off-switch of the world.\n\nZero and infinity are related, and the relation is a deep one, and it is the reason mathematicians have spent centuries thinking about both. Divide a number by a number that gets smaller and smaller, closer and closer to zero, and the answer gets larger and larger, closer and closer to infinity, and the zero and the infinity are two ends of the same idea: the smallest thing there is, and the largest. The zero is the beginning of counting, and infinity is the end of it, and the two of them, together, frame the whole of mathematics, and the whole of the world that mathematics describes.\n\nThe symbol itself, the small circle, is worth a last word, because it is the same in every language, and it is understood everywhere, and it needs no translation. A zero written in London is a zero written in Delhi, and a zero written in Delhi is a zero written in Beijing, and the circle is the same, and the meaning is the same, and the idea is the same. The zero is the most universal symbol ever invented, and it is a circle, and a circle has no beginning and no end, and it is empty in the middle, and it is the perfect shape for the idea it carries: nothing, and everything that nothing makes possible.\n\nThe history of zero is a short history, as histories go, and it is a history of an idea that was invented, and resisted, and adopted, and that changed everything. It is a reminder that the most powerful ideas are often the simplest, and that the simplest idea of all, the idea that nothing can be something, took the longest to accept, and did the most when it was accepted. The next time you write a zero, think of the centuries it took to get there, and of the merchants who used it in secret, and of the scholars who feared it, and of the mathematicians who trusted it, and of the world that was built on it, out of nothing, and out of a small circle, drawn in the sand, that said: there is nothing here, and the nothing matters.\n"
