Full text

How a Bicycle Stays Upright By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

A bicycle stays upright, and it should not. It is two wheels, in a line, with a person on top, and it is, by every rule of balance, an impossible machine. A table with two legs falls over. A chair with two legs falls over. A bicycle with two wheels, in a line, should fall over, and it does, when it is standing still, and when it is pushed without a rider. But when it is ridden, and when it is moving, it stays up, and the rider, who is doing nothing that looks like balancing, stays up with it, and the two of them travel, together, at speed, around corners, and over bumps, and the machine does not fall. How it does this is a story about steering, and about leaning, and about a machine that corrects itself faster than its rider can think.

The first thing to understand is that a bicycle is not balanced. It is constantly falling, and constantly being caught, and the catching is done by steering. When a bicycle leans to the left, it begins to fall to the left, and the fall, if nothing is done, will end on the ground. But the bicycle, as it falls, turns its front wheel toward the fall, and the turning moves the wheels back under the rider, and the fall is caught, and the bicycle is upright again, for a moment, until it leans the other way, and falls the other way, and is caught again. The bicycle stays upright by falling, left and right, in small, quick falls, each one caught by a small, quick turn of the wheel. The rider does not feel the falling, because the falls are small, and the catches are fast, and the whole thing feels like balance, when it is really a series of rescues.

The steering that catches the fall is not done by the rider's hands, or not only by them. The front wheel of a bicycle is not fixed in a straight line. It is set at an angle, leaning back, so that the point where the wheel touches the ground is behind the point where the steering axis meets the ground. This trailing, called the trail, is the bicycle's secret. When the bicycle leans, the weight of the rider presses down through the steering axis, and the wheel, because its contact point trails behind, is pulled around, toward the lean, by the weight itself. The bicycle steers itself into the fall, without the rider doing anything, and the steering is the work of the geometry, not of the hands. The hands are there to help, and to choose, but the machine does most of the work on its own.

The rider helps, of course, and the help is mostly a matter of leaning. To turn a corner, the rider does not turn the handlebars. The rider leans, into the corner, and the lean makes the bicycle fall toward the corner, and the bicycle steers into the fall, and the turn happens, and the rider, who is leaning, and falling, and being caught, all at once, feels as if they are steering, when they are really falling, in a controlled way, around a corner. The lean is the steering, and the steering is the lean, and the two are one thing, done by the body, not by the hands. A child learning to ride does not learn to steer. The child learns to lean, and to trust the lean, and to let the bicycle catch the fall, and the learning is a matter of courage, more than skill.

Speed matters, and it matters because the catching gets easier as the bicycle goes faster. A slow bicycle is hard to ride, because the falls are slow, and the catches are slow, and the rider must work hard to keep the wheels under them. A fast bicycle is easy to ride, because the falls are fast, and the catches are fast, and the wheels, spinning, help to keep the machine upright. The spinning wheels have a gyroscopic effect, a resistance to being tilted, and the effect is real, but it is small, and it is not the main reason the bicycle stays up. The main reason is the steering, and the leaning, and the catching, and the speed makes the catching quicker, and the quicker the catching, the easier the ride.

The gyroscopic effect deserves a word, because it is the explanation most people have heard, and it is mostly wrong. A spinning wheel does resist being tilted, and the resistance is called gyroscopic precession, and it is a real force, and it does help, a little. But a bicycle with wheels that do not spin, or with wheels made of a material that has no gyroscopic effect, can still be ridden, and can still be balanced, and the balance is still done by steering, and by leaning, and by the geometry of the front wheel. The gyroscope is a helper, not the hero. The hero is the trail, and the lean, and the fall, and the catch.

The rider's body is part of the machine, and the rider's body is the largest part of it. The bicycle weighs a few kilograms. The rider weighs ten times as much, and the rider's weight is high up, on a narrow saddle, and the rider's movements, a shift of the hips, a turn of the shoulders, a lean of the head, move the centre of gravity, and the centre of gravity is the thing that must be kept over the wheels. The rider balances the bicycle the way a person balances a broom on a fingertip, by moving the support under the weight, and the support is the wheels, and the weight is the rider, and the moving is done by steering, and by leaning, and by the small, constant adjustments that the rider makes without thinking, and that the bicycle makes without being asked.

Learning to ride is learning to trust the fall, and the trust is hard to learn, because the fall feels like failure, and the catch feels like luck. A child on a bicycle, wobbling, with a parent running behind, is learning that the lean is not the end, and that the fall can be caught, and that the catching is the riding. The parent, who holds the saddle, is doing the steering for the child, and the child, who does not know it, is learning to lean, and one day the parent lets go, and the child leans, and falls, and is caught, by the bicycle, and by the lean, and the child is riding, and the parent is running, and the child is gone, down the road, on a machine that should fall over, and does not.

The bicycle is also a machine for going, and the going is part of the staying up. A bicycle at rest falls over, because there is no motion to catch the fall, and no speed to make the catching quick. A bicycle in motion stays up, because the motion is the catching, and the catching is the motion, and the two cannot be separated. The bicycle teaches a lesson that is larger than itself: that balance is not a state, but a process, and that the process is a series of small falls, caught in time, and that the catching is the balance, and that the one who is afraid to fall will never learn to ride.

The corners are the test of the bicycle, and the test is passed by leaning further, and by trusting more. A rider who is afraid to lean will go wide, and will run out of road, and a rider who leans too far will fall, and the line between the two is thin, and it is learned by practice, and by falling, and by getting up, and by trying again. The bicycle does not forgive a rider who will not lean, and it does not forgive a rider who leans too much, and it rewards the rider who leans just enough, and who trusts the machine to catch the fall, and the trust is earned, ride by ride, corner by corner, until the leaning is as natural as walking.

The bumps are the other test, and they are passed by the rider's legs, and by the bicycle's frame, and by the tyres, which are the bicycle's suspension. A bump lifts the wheel, and the wheel, when it comes down, must come down under the rider, and the rider, who has learned to stand on the pedals, lets the bicycle move beneath them, and the bicycle, which is light, moves, and the rider, who is heavy, stays, and the two meet again, on the other side of the bump, and the ride goes on. The bicycle is a machine that moves, and the rider is a person who moves with it, and the two together are a single thing, balanced, and falling, and caught, and moving, all at once.

The chain and the gears are the bicycle's other machine, and they deserve a word, because they are what turn the rider's legs into motion. The chain runs from the pedals to the back wheel, and the gears change the ratio between them, so that a slow turn of the pedals can become a fast turn of the wheel, or a fast turn of the pedals can become a slow, strong turn of the wheel, for a hill. The gears are the bicycle's gearbox, and they are worked by the rider's hands, and they let the rider keep pedalling at the same comfortable pace, whether the road is flat, or uphill, or downhill. The chain is a simple thing, and it is efficient, and it loses very little of the rider's effort, and it is the reason a bicycle is the most efficient machine ever made for moving a person.

The brakes are the bicycle's safety, and they are simple too: a pad, pressed against the rim of the wheel, or a disc, gripped by a caliper, and the pressing turns the wheel's motion into heat, and the heat is thrown away, into the air, and the bicycle slows, and stops. The brakes are the rider's way of saying no, and they are used sparingly, by a good rider, because a bicycle that is braked is a bicycle that is not moving, and a bicycle that is not moving falls over. The good rider brakes before the corner, and not in it, and coasts through, leaning, and pedals out, and the braking is part of the riding, and the riding is part of the balance.

The track stand is the bicycle's party trick, and it is the proof of the whole story. A skilled rider can stand still, on a bicycle, without putting a foot down, by turning the front wheel, and by shifting their weight, and by making the small, quick corrections that the moving bicycle makes by itself. The track stand is balance without motion, and it is hard, and it is tiring, and it is the exception that proves the rule: that the bicycle stays up by moving, and by falling, and by being caught, and that standing still is the hardest thing of all, because there is no motion to catch the fall.

The bicycle's history is a short one, and it is worth a paragraph, because it shows how quickly a good idea can spread. The first bicycles were wooden, and they were pushed along with the feet, and they were called hobby horses, and they were a toy for the rich. The first bicycles with pedals came a generation later, and they were heavy, and they were called boneshakers, for good reason, and they were ridden by young men, and they were laughed at. The bicycle as we know it, with two wheels of the same size, and a chain, and pneumatic tyres, came at the end of the nineteenth century, and it spread, in a decade, all over the world, and it gave women freedom, and workers mobility, and children a machine of their own, and it changed the world, and it did it on two wheels, in a line, that should not stay up, and do.

That is how a bicycle stays upright: not by being balanced, but by falling, and by being caught, by a wheel that steers itself into the fall, by a rider who leans into the turn, by a geometry that turns a lean into a steer, and by a speed that makes the catching quick. It is a machine that should fall over, and it does not, because it is always falling, and always being caught, and the falling and the catching, done fast enough, and small enough, look like balance, and feel like balance, and are, for all practical purposes, balance. Ride it, and lean, and trust the fall, and the bicycle will catch you, as it has caught a million riders before you, on a machine of two wheels, in a line, that should not stay up, and does.

# Program: How a Bicycle Stays Upright
TEXT = "How a Bicycle Stays Upright\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nA bicycle stays upright, and it should not. It is two wheels, in a line, with a person on top, and it is, by every rule of balance, an impossible machine. A table with two legs falls over. A chair with two legs falls over. A bicycle with two wheels, in a line, should fall over, and it does, when it is standing still, and when it is pushed without a rider. But when it is ridden, and when it is moving, it stays up, and the rider, who is doing nothing that looks like balancing, stays up with it, and the two of them travel, together, at speed, around corners, and over bumps, and the machine does not fall. How it does this is a story about steering, and about leaning, and about a machine that corrects itself faster than its rider can think.\n\nThe first thing to understand is that a bicycle is not balanced. It is constantly falling, and constantly being caught, and the catching is done by steering. When a bicycle leans to the left, it begins to fall to the left, and the fall, if nothing is done, will end on the ground. But the bicycle, as it falls, turns its front wheel toward the fall, and the turning moves the wheels back under the rider, and the fall is caught, and the bicycle is upright again, for a moment, until it leans the other way, and falls the other way, and is caught again. The bicycle stays upright by falling, left and right, in small, quick falls, each one caught by a small, quick turn of the wheel. The rider does not feel the falling, because the falls are small, and the catches are fast, and the whole thing feels like balance, when it is really a series of rescues.\n\nThe steering that catches the fall is not done by the rider's hands, or not only by them. The front wheel of a bicycle is not fixed in a straight line. It is set at an angle, leaning back, so that the point where the wheel touches the ground is behind the point where the steering axis meets the ground. This trailing, called the trail, is the bicycle's secret. When the bicycle leans, the weight of the rider presses down through the steering axis, and the wheel, because its contact point trails behind, is pulled around, toward the lean, by the weight itself. The bicycle steers itself into the fall, without the rider doing anything, and the steering is the work of the geometry, not of the hands. The hands are there to help, and to choose, but the machine does most of the work on its own.\n\nThe rider helps, of course, and the help is mostly a matter of leaning. To turn a corner, the rider does not turn the handlebars. The rider leans, into the corner, and the lean makes the bicycle fall toward the corner, and the bicycle steers into the fall, and the turn happens, and the rider, who is leaning, and falling, and being caught, all at once, feels as if they are steering, when they are really falling, in a controlled way, around a corner. The lean is the steering, and the steering is the lean, and the two are one thing, done by the body, not by the hands. A child learning to ride does not learn to steer. The child learns to lean, and to trust the lean, and to let the bicycle catch the fall, and the learning is a matter of courage, more than skill.\n\nSpeed matters, and it matters because the catching gets easier as the bicycle goes faster. A slow bicycle is hard to ride, because the falls are slow, and the catches are slow, and the rider must work hard to keep the wheels under them. A fast bicycle is easy to ride, because the falls are fast, and the catches are fast, and the wheels, spinning, help to keep the machine upright. The spinning wheels have a gyroscopic effect, a resistance to being tilted, and the effect is real, but it is small, and it is not the main reason the bicycle stays up. The main reason is the steering, and the leaning, and the catching, and the speed makes the catching quicker, and the quicker the catching, the easier the ride.\n\nThe gyroscopic effect deserves a word, because it is the explanation most people have heard, and it is mostly wrong. A spinning wheel does resist being tilted, and the resistance is called gyroscopic precession, and it is a real force, and it does help, a little. But a bicycle with wheels that do not spin, or with wheels made of a material that has no gyroscopic effect, can still be ridden, and can still be balanced, and the balance is still done by steering, and by leaning, and by the geometry of the front wheel. The gyroscope is a helper, not the hero. The hero is the trail, and the lean, and the fall, and the catch.\n\nThe rider's body is part of the machine, and the rider's body is the largest part of it. The bicycle weighs a few kilograms. The rider weighs ten times as much, and the rider's weight is high up, on a narrow saddle, and the rider's movements, a shift of the hips, a turn of the shoulders, a lean of the head, move the centre of gravity, and the centre of gravity is the thing that must be kept over the wheels. The rider balances the bicycle the way a person balances a broom on a fingertip, by moving the support under the weight, and the support is the wheels, and the weight is the rider, and the moving is done by steering, and by leaning, and by the small, constant adjustments that the rider makes without thinking, and that the bicycle makes without being asked.\n\nLearning to ride is learning to trust the fall, and the trust is hard to learn, because the fall feels like failure, and the catch feels like luck. A child on a bicycle, wobbling, with a parent running behind, is learning that the lean is not the end, and that the fall can be caught, and that the catching is the riding. The parent, who holds the saddle, is doing the steering for the child, and the child, who does not know it, is learning to lean, and one day the parent lets go, and the child leans, and falls, and is caught, by the bicycle, and by the lean, and the child is riding, and the parent is running, and the child is gone, down the road, on a machine that should fall over, and does not.\n\nThe bicycle is also a machine for going, and the going is part of the staying up. A bicycle at rest falls over, because there is no motion to catch the fall, and no speed to make the catching quick. A bicycle in motion stays up, because the motion is the catching, and the catching is the motion, and the two cannot be separated. The bicycle teaches a lesson that is larger than itself: that balance is not a state, but a process, and that the process is a series of small falls, caught in time, and that the catching is the balance, and that the one who is afraid to fall will never learn to ride.\n\nThe corners are the test of the bicycle, and the test is passed by leaning further, and by trusting more. A rider who is afraid to lean will go wide, and will run out of road, and a rider who leans too far will fall, and the line between the two is thin, and it is learned by practice, and by falling, and by getting up, and by trying again. The bicycle does not forgive a rider who will not lean, and it does not forgive a rider who leans too much, and it rewards the rider who leans just enough, and who trusts the machine to catch the fall, and the trust is earned, ride by ride, corner by corner, until the leaning is as natural as walking.\n\nThe bumps are the other test, and they are passed by the rider's legs, and by the bicycle's frame, and by the tyres, which are the bicycle's suspension. A bump lifts the wheel, and the wheel, when it comes down, must come down under the rider, and the rider, who has learned to stand on the pedals, lets the bicycle move beneath them, and the bicycle, which is light, moves, and the rider, who is heavy, stays, and the two meet again, on the other side of the bump, and the ride goes on. The bicycle is a machine that moves, and the rider is a person who moves with it, and the two together are a single thing, balanced, and falling, and caught, and moving, all at once.\n\nThe chain and the gears are the bicycle's other machine, and they deserve a word, because they are what turn the rider's legs into motion. The chain runs from the pedals to the back wheel, and the gears change the ratio between them, so that a slow turn of the pedals can become a fast turn of the wheel, or a fast turn of the pedals can become a slow, strong turn of the wheel, for a hill. The gears are the bicycle's gearbox, and they are worked by the rider's hands, and they let the rider keep pedalling at the same comfortable pace, whether the road is flat, or uphill, or downhill. The chain is a simple thing, and it is efficient, and it loses very little of the rider's effort, and it is the reason a bicycle is the most efficient machine ever made for moving a person.\n\nThe brakes are the bicycle's safety, and they are simple too: a pad, pressed against the rim of the wheel, or a disc, gripped by a caliper, and the pressing turns the wheel's motion into heat, and the heat is thrown away, into the air, and the bicycle slows, and stops. The brakes are the rider's way of saying no, and they are used sparingly, by a good rider, because a bicycle that is braked is a bicycle that is not moving, and a bicycle that is not moving falls over. The good rider brakes before the corner, and not in it, and coasts through, leaning, and pedals out, and the braking is part of the riding, and the riding is part of the balance.\n\nThe track stand is the bicycle's party trick, and it is the proof of the whole story. A skilled rider can stand still, on a bicycle, without putting a foot down, by turning the front wheel, and by shifting their weight, and by making the small, quick corrections that the moving bicycle makes by itself. The track stand is balance without motion, and it is hard, and it is tiring, and it is the exception that proves the rule: that the bicycle stays up by moving, and by falling, and by being caught, and that standing still is the hardest thing of all, because there is no motion to catch the fall.\n\nThe bicycle's history is a short one, and it is worth a paragraph, because it shows how quickly a good idea can spread. The first bicycles were wooden, and they were pushed along with the feet, and they were called hobby horses, and they were a toy for the rich. The first bicycles with pedals came a generation later, and they were heavy, and they were called boneshakers, for good reason, and they were ridden by young men, and they were laughed at. The bicycle as we know it, with two wheels of the same size, and a chain, and pneumatic tyres, came at the end of the nineteenth century, and it spread, in a decade, all over the world, and it gave women freedom, and workers mobility, and children a machine of their own, and it changed the world, and it did it on two wheels, in a line, that should not stay up, and do.\n\nThat is how a bicycle stays upright: not by being balanced, but by falling, and by being caught, by a wheel that steers itself into the fall, by a rider who leans into the turn, by a geometry that turns a lean into a steer, and by a speed that makes the catching quick. It is a machine that should fall over, and it does not, because it is always falling, and always being caught, and the falling and the catching, done fast enough, and small enough, look like balance, and feel like balance, and are, for all practical purposes, balance. Ride it, and lean, and trust the fall, and the bicycle will catch you, as it has caught a million riders before you, on a machine of two wheels, in a line, that should not stay up, and does.\n"
import re, math

Full text

The Beekeeper's Almanac: Twelve Months with the Hives By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

January is the quiet month, and the beekeeper, in January, does little, and listens much. The hives stand in a row, in the field, under a grey sky, and the bees are inside, in a cluster, keeping warm, and the beekeeper walks the row, and listens, with an ear to the wood, for the low hum that says the colony is alive, and he lifts the roof, briefly, and checks the weight of the hive, by lifting one side, and the weight says whether the bees have enough food to last the winter, and if they do not, he gives them fondant, a cake of sugar, placed on top of the frames, and he closes the roof, and he goes home, and he waits for February.

February is the month of the first flights, on the mild days, when the sun comes out, and the bees come out, to empty themselves, and to look for the first flowers, and the beekeeper watches them, and counts them, and notes which hives are flying, and which are not, and the ones that are not flying are a worry, and he listens to them, and he checks their weight, and he hopes. The snowdrops are out, and the crocuses, and the bees work them, for the first pollen of the year, and the pollen is the protein the colony needs, to raise the first brood, and the beekeeper, watching the bees come home with their baskets full of yellow pollen, knows that the year has begun.

March is the month of the first inspection, on a warm day, when the wind has dropped, and the beekeeper opens the hive, and looks at the frames, and finds the queen, or finds the eggs she has laid, which is as good as finding her, and he checks the brood, and the stores, and the space, and he cleans the floor, and he closes the hive, and he writes it all in the notebook, in the columns, hive by hive, and the notebook is the almanac, and the almanac is the memory of the year.

April is the month of growth, and the colony grows fast, and the beekeeper adds a super, a box on top of the hive, for the honey, and the bees fill it, and the beekeeper adds another, and the colony, which was a handful of bees in March, is a crowd in April, and the crowd needs room, and the beekeeper gives it room, and the room is the super, and the super is the honey, and the honey is the year's work, begun.

May is the month of swarming, and the beekeeper watches for it, and listens for it, and the signs are the queen cells, the peanut-shaped cells on the edge of the comb, in which a new queen is being raised, and the beekeeper, finding them, must decide: to let the colony swarm, and lose half his bees, or to split the colony, and make a new hive, and keep the bees, and the decision is the beekeeper's art, and it is learned by doing, and by losing a swarm, and by catching one, and by learning, year by year, what the bees will do, and what the beekeeper can do about it.

June is the month of the honey flow, when the flowers are out, and the bees are working, from dawn to dusk, and the supers fill, and the beekeeper watches the weight of the hives, and the supers, and the bees, coming and going, in a stream, and the stream is the honey, being made, and the beekeeper, standing by the hives, can hear the hum, and can smell the honey, and can see the year's work, in the making, and the work is good.

July is the month of the harvest, and the beekeeper takes the supers, full of honey, and he uncaps the comb, and he spins the frames, in the extractor, and the honey flies out, and runs down, into a bucket, and the bucket fills, and the honey is strained, and settled, and jarred, and the jars are labelled, and the labels say the year, and the place, and the flowers, and the honey is the year's work, made visible, and the beekeeper, tasting it, knows the year, in a spoonful.

August is the month of the varroa, the mite that lives on the bees, and that weakens them, and that must be treated, and the beekeeper treats it, with a medicine, or with a method, and he checks the mite fall, on a board under the hive, and he counts the mites, and the count tells him whether the treatment has worked, and whether the colony will be strong enough for the winter. The varroa is the beekeeper's enemy, and it is a small enemy, and a persistent one, and the fight against it is the fight of the modern beekeeper, and it is fought, year after year, with care, and with counting.

September is the month of the feeding, when the beekeeper gives the bees the sugar syrup they will need for the winter, and he feeds them, in the evening, when the flying has stopped, and the syrup is taken down, into the comb, and stored, and the stores are checked, and the weight of the hive is checked, and the hive that is light is fed more, and the hive that is heavy is left alone, and the feeding is the beekeeper's gift to the bees, and the bees' gift, in return, is the spring.

October is the month of the putting away, when the beekeeper takes off the empty supers, and stores them, and puts on the mouse guards, small grilles over the entrance, to keep the mice out, and he checks the roofs, and the stands, and he tidies the apiary, and he puts the tools away, and he writes up the notebook, and the notebook says how the year went, and which hives did well, and which did not, and why.

November is the month of the woodwork, when the beekeeper, indoors, repairs the hives, and makes new frames, and paints the boxes, and orders the queens for next year, and reads, and plans, and the work is quiet, and it is the work of the winter, and it is the work that makes the next year possible. The beekeeper, in November, is a carpenter, and a planner, and a reader, and the bees, outside, are a cluster, in the dark, keeping warm.

December is the month of the oxalic acid, a treatment for the varroa, given on a cold day, when the colony is broodless, and the mites are exposed, and the beekeeper trickles the acid, or vaporises it, and the mites fall, and the colony is clean, for the winter, and the beekeeper closes the hive, and goes home, for Christmas, and the year is over, and the next year is already begun, in the cluster, in the dark, in the hive, waiting for January, and the first flights, and the snowdrops, and the crocuses, and the year, again.

The queen is the heart of the colony, and she is worth a paragraph, because the colony is hers, in a sense, and she is the colony's, in another. The queen is the only bee in the hive that lays eggs, and she lays them by the thousand, a thousand a day, in the summer, and the colony is her children, and her grandchildren, and the hive is her family, and the family is the colony. The beekeeper marks her, with a dot of paint, in the colour of the year, so that she can be found, and he finds her, or finds her eggs, at every inspection, because a colony without a queen, or without eggs, is a colony that is dying, and the beekeeper must know, and must act, and the queen is the first thing he looks for, and the last thing he forgets.

The dance is the bees' language, and it is one of the wonders of the natural world. A bee that has found flowers comes home, and dances, on the comb, in the dark, and the dance tells the other bees where the flowers are, and how far away, and in which direction, and the other bees follow the dance, and fly to the flowers, and the colony feeds, because a bee danced. The beekeeper cannot see the dance, in the dark of the hive, but he knows it is happening, because the bees come home with pollen, and the pollen is the proof, and the dance is the reason, and the beekeeper, who watches the entrance, sees the proof, and trusts the reason.

The honeybee is not the only bee, and the beekeeper knows it, and respects it. The bumblebee, fat and furry, works the flowers the honeybee cannot, and the solitary bee, small and quiet, works the garden, and the beekeeper, who keeps honeybees, knows that the honeybee is a guest in the garden, and that the wild bees were there first, and that the garden needs them all. The beekeeper plants flowers for the wild bees, as well as for his own, and he leaves a corner of the garden wild, for the solitary bees, and the bumblebees, and the garden is better for it, and the honey is better for it, because a garden with many bees is a garden with many flowers, and many flowers make much honey.

The veil and the suit are the beekeeper's armour, and they are worn, always, at the hive, because a bee sting is a small thing, and a hundred bee stings are not. The veil is a mesh, over the face, and the suit is white, because white is calm to the bees, and the gloves are leather, and the boots are rubber, and the beekeeper, dressed in white, moves slowly, and gently, at the hive, because a sudden movement alarms the bees, and an alarmed bee stings, and a stinging bee dies, and the beekeeper does not want the bees to die, and the bees do not want to sting, and the suit is the compromise between them.

The swarm, when it comes, is a sight, and it is a sound, and it is the colony dividing, and the beekeeper, who has lost a swarm, will chase it, with a box, and a ladder, and a veil, and he will catch it, if he can, and bring it home, to a new hive, and the swarm, which is a cloud of bees, hanging from a branch, in a ball, will go into the box, and into the hive, and will become a new colony, and the beekeeper will have a new hive, and the year will have a new beginning, in the middle of the summer.

The winter cluster is the bees' miracle, and it is worth a paragraph, because it is how the colony survives the cold. The bees gather, in a ball, in the hive, and the ball is warm, in the middle, where the queen is, and cold, on the outside, where the bees take turns, moving in, and out, and the cluster shivers, and the shivering makes heat, and the heat keeps the queen alive, and the colony alive, and the beekeeper, listening, with his ear to the wood, hears the hum, and knows the cluster is there, and alive, and waiting for the spring.

And the neighbours matter, because a beekeeper's bees are not only the beekeeper's. The bees fly, for miles, and they visit the neighbours' gardens, and the neighbours' flowers, and the neighbours, who may love the bees, or fear them, must be considered, and the beekeeper considers them, and puts the hives where the flight path is high, and gives the neighbours honey, at Christmas, and the honey is the beekeeper's diplomacy, and it works, year after year, because a jar of honey is a hard thing to argue with.

The almanac is the beekeeper's year, written in the notebook, hive by hive, month by month, and it is a record of the bees, and of the weather, and of the flowers, and of the work, and it is the beekeeper's memory, and the memory is the craft. A beekeeper who keeps an almanac is a beekeeper who learns, year by year, and who does not make the same mistake twice, and who knows, when the spring comes, what to expect, and what to do, and the almanac is the difference between a beekeeper and a person who keeps bees.

The bees, for their part, do not know the almanac, and they do not need it. They know the year, in their own way, by the light, and the warmth, and the flowers, and they do the work of the year, without a notebook, and without a plan, and they do it well, and the beekeeper, who watches them, and who writes it down, is the one who learns, and the learning is the craft, and the craft is the keeping, and the keeping is the year, twelve months, with the hives, from January to December, and back to January again.

The varroa is the beekeeper's enemy, and it is a small enemy, and a persistent one, and the fight against it is the fight of the modern beekeeper, and it is fought, year after year, with care, and with counting.\n\nSeptember is the month of the feeding, when the beekeeper gives the bees the sugar syrup they will need for the winter, and he feeds them, in the evening, when the flying has stopped, and the syrup is taken down, into the comb, and stored, and the stores are checked, and the weight of the hive is checked, and the hive that is light is fed more, and the hive that is heavy is left alone, and the feeding is the beekeeper's gift to the bees, and the bees' gift, in return, is the spring.\n\nOctober is the month of the putting away, when the beekeeper takes off the empty supers, and stores them, and puts on the mouse guards, small grilles over the entrance, to keep the mice out, and he checks the roofs, and the stands, and he tidies the apiary, and he puts the tools away, and he writes up the notebook, and the notebook says how the year went, and which hives did well, and which did not, and why.\n\nNovember is the month of the woodwork, when the beekeeper, indoors, repairs the hives, and makes new frames, and paints the boxes, and orders the queens for next year, and reads, and plans, and the work is quiet, and it is the work of the winter, and it is the work that makes the next year possible. The beekeeper, in November, is a carpenter, and a planner, and a reader, and the bees, outside, are a cluster, in the dark, keeping warm.\n\nDecember is the month of the oxalic acid, a treatment for the varroa, given on a cold day, when the colony is broodless, and the mites are exposed, and the beekeeper trickles the acid, or vaporises it, and the mites fall, and the colony is clean, for the winter, and the beekeeper closes the hive, and goes home, for Christmas, and the year is over, and the next year is already begun, in the cluster, in the dark, in the hive, waiting for January, and the first flights, and the snowdrops, and the crocuses, and the year, again.\n\nThe queen is the heart of the colony, and she is worth a paragraph, because the colony is hers, in a sense, and she is the colony's, in another. The queen is the only bee in the hive that lays eggs, and she lays them by the thousand, a thousand a day, in the summer, and the colony is her children, and her grandchildren, and the hive is her family, and the family is the colony. The beekeeper marks her, with a dot of paint, in the colour of the year, so that she can be found, and he finds her, or finds her eggs, at every inspection, because a colony without a queen, or without eggs, is a colony that is dying, and the beekeeper must know, and must act, and the queen is the first thing he looks for, and the last thing he forgets.\n\nThe dance is the bees' language, and it is one of the wonders of the natural world. A bee that has found flowers comes home, and dances, on the comb, in the dark, and the dance tells the other bees where the flowers are, and how far away, and in which direction, and the other bees follow the dance, and fly to the flowers, and the colony feeds, because a bee danced. The beekeeper cannot see the dance, in the dark of the hive, but he knows it is happening, because the bees come home with pollen, and the pollen is the proof, and the dance is the reason, and the beekeeper, who watches the entrance, sees the proof, and trusts the reason.\n\nThe honeybee is not the only bee, and the beekeeper knows it, and respects it. The bumblebee, fat and furry, works the flowers the honeybee cannot, and the solitary bee, small and quiet, works the garden, and the beekeeper, who keeps honeybees, knows that the honeybee is a guest in the garden, and that the wild bees were there first, and that the garden needs them all. The beekeeper plants flowers for the wild bees, as well as for his own, and he leaves a corner of the garden wild, for the solitary bees, and the bumblebees, and the garden is better for it, and the honey is better for it, because a garden with many bees is a garden with many flowers, and many flowers make much honey.\n\nThe veil and the suit are the beekeeper's armour, and they are worn, always, at the hive, because a bee sting is a small thing, and a hundred bee stings are not. The veil is a mesh, over the face, and the suit is white, because white is calm to the bees, and the gloves are leather, and the boots are rubber, and the beekeeper, dressed in white, moves slowly, and gently, at the hive, because a sudden movement alarms the bees, and an alarmed bee stings, and a stinging bee dies, and the beekeeper does not want the bees to die, and the bees do not want to sting, and the suit is the compromise between them.\n\nThe swarm, when it comes, is a sight, and it is a sound, and it is the colony dividing, and the beekeeper, who has lost a swarm, will chase it, with a box, and a ladder, and a veil, and he will catch it, if he can, and bring it home, to a new hive, and the swarm, which is a cloud of bees, hanging from a branch, in a ball, will go into the box, and into the hive, and will become a new colony, and the beekeeper will have a new hive, and the year will have a new beginning, in the middle of the summer.\n\nThe winter cluster is the bees' miracle, and it is worth a paragraph, because it is how the colony survives the cold. The bees gather, in a ball, in the hive, and the ball is warm, in the middle, where the queen is, and cold, on the outside, where the bees take turns, moving in, and out, and the cluster shivers, and the shivering makes heat, and the heat keeps the queen alive, and the colony alive, and the beekeeper, listening, with his ear to the wood, hears the hum, and knows the cluster is there, and alive, and waiting for the spring.\n\nAnd the neighbours matter, because a beekeeper's bees are not only the beekeeper's. The bees fly, for miles, and they visit the neighbours' gardens, and the neighbours' flowers, and the neighbours, who may love the bees, or fear them, must be considered, and the beekeeper considers them, and puts the hives where the flight path is high, and gives the neighbours honey, at Christmas, and the honey is the beekeeper's diplomacy, and it works, year after year, because a jar of honey is a hard thing to argue with.\n\nThe almanac is the beekeeper's year, written in the notebook, hive by hive, month by month, and it is a record of the bees, and of the weather, and of the flowers, and of the work, and it is the beekeeper's memory, and the memory is the craft. A beekeeper who keeps an almanac is a beekeeper who learns, year by year, and who does not make the same mistake twice, and who knows, when the spring comes, what to expect, and what to do, and the almanac is the difference between a beekeeper and a person who keeps bees.\n\nThe bees, for their part, do not know the almanac, and they do not need it. They know the year, in their own way, by the light, and the warmth, and the flowers, and they do the work of the year, without a notebook, and without a plan, and they do it well, and the beekeeper, who watches them, and who writes it down, is the one who learns, and the learning is the craft, and the craft is the keeping, and the keeping is the year, twelve months, with the hives, from January to December, and back to January again.\n"

# Program: The Beekeeper's Almanac: Twelve Months with the Hives
TEXT = "The Beekeeper's Almanac: Twelve Months with the Hives\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nJanuary is the quiet month, and the beekeeper, in January, does little, and listens much. The hives stand in a row, in the field, under a grey sky, and the bees are inside, in a cluster, keeping warm, and the beekeeper walks the row, and listens, with an ear to the wood, for the low hum that says the colony is alive, and he lifts the roof, briefly, and checks the weight of the hive, by lifting one side, and the weight says whether the bees have enough food to last the winter, and if they do not, he gives them fondant, a cake of sugar, placed on top of the frames, and he closes the roof, and he goes home, and he waits for February.\n\nFebruary is the month of the first flights, on the mild days, when the sun comes out, and the bees come out, to empty themselves, and to look for the first flowers, and the beekeeper watches them, and counts them, and notes which hives are flying, and which are not, and the ones that are not flying are a worry, and he listens to them, and he checks their weight, and he hopes. The snowdrops are out, and the crocuses, and the bees work them, for the first pollen of the year, and the pollen is the protein the colony needs, to raise the first brood, and the beekeeper, watching the bees come home with their baskets full of yellow pollen, knows that the year has begun.\n\nMarch is the month of the first inspection, on a warm day, when the wind has dropped, and the beekeeper opens the hive, and looks at the frames, and finds the queen, or finds the eggs she has laid, which is as good as finding her, and he checks the brood, and the stores, and the space, and he cleans the floor, and he closes the hive, and he writes it all in the notebook, in the columns, hive by hive, and the notebook is the almanac, and the almanac is the memory of the year.\n\nApril is the month of growth, and the colony grows fast, and the beekeeper adds a super, a box on top of the hive, for the honey, and the bees fill it, and the beekeeper adds another, and the colony, which was a handful of bees in March, is a crowd in April, and the crowd needs room, and the beekeeper gives it room, and the room is the super, and the super is the honey, and the honey is the year's work, begun.\n\nMay is Full text

Walking the Old Canal Path By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

The canal in this article is an illustrative one, drawn from the ordinary features of old canals in many places. No single real canal is described.

The old canal path begins at the bridge, where the road crosses the water, and where the towpath drops down, beside the canal, and the walking begins. The path is narrow, and it is worn, and it is lined with grass, and with the occasional tree, and it runs beside the water, in a straight line, for as far as the eye can see, and the walking is easy, because the canal is flat, and the path is flat, and the only work is the walking itself, and the looking.

The canal was built for boats, and the boats were built for the canal, and the two together were the freight of their age. The canal is a ditch, dug by hand, and lined with clay, and filled with water, and the boats that used it were long, and narrow, and loaded with coal, and with stone, and with grain, and with timber, and they were pulled by horses, along the towpath, and the horses walked, and the boats followed, at the pace of a walk, and the goods of the country moved, slowly, and steadily, along the water.

The locks are the canal's stairs, and they are the most interesting thing on the path. A lock is a chamber, with gates at each end, and the chamber is filled, or emptied, to raise a boat, or to lower it, from one level of the canal to the next. The gates are heavy, and they are worked by hand, by a beam, pushed by a person, and the water is let in, or let out, by a paddle, wound up by a windlass, and the boat rises, or falls, slowly, in the chamber, and the person at the gates watches it, and the watching is the work, and the work is slow, and it is the reason the canal moves at the pace of a walk.

The bridges are the canal's crossings, and they are small, and arched, and made of brick, and they carry the lanes, and the footpaths, over the water, and they are numbered, and the numbers are painted on them, in white, and the numbers are the canal's milestones, and the walker, counting them, knows how far they have come, and how far they have to go. The bridges are also the canal's roofs, in a sense, because the boats pass under them, and the horses pass over them, and the towpath passes beside them, and the whole of the canal's life passes through them, in one way or another.

The aqueduct is the canal's boldest piece, and it is the place where the canal crosses a valley, or a river, on a bridge of its own, carrying the water, in a trough, high above the ground, and the walker, crossing it, looks down, and sees the valley below, and the river, and the road, and the canal, above them all, a river in the sky, held up by arches, and by iron, and by the confidence of the men who built it. The aqueduct is a wonder, and it is a wonder that has stood for two hundred years, carrying water, and boats, and walkers, above the valley, as if it were the most natural thing in the world.

The tunnels are the canal's secret places, and they are dark, and long, and wet, and the boats that used them were pulled through by men, lying on their backs, on the roof of the boat, pushing against the roof of the tunnel with their feet, in the dark, in a practice called legging, and the leggers worked in the dark, and in the wet, and they were paid by the boat, and they were strong, and they were the canal's hidden workforce. The tunnels are closed now, to boats, and the towpath goes over them, or around them, and the walker, passing the tunnel mouth, looks into the dark, and imagines the leggers, and the boats, and the dark, and the water.

The wildlife of the canal is its own, and it is the reason the path is worth walking. The kingfisher flashes blue, along the water, and the heron stands, still, in the shallows, and the moorhens paddle, in the reeds, and the dragonflies hover, over the water, in the summer, and the fish rise, in the evening, and the canal, which was built for boats, has become a home for the wild, and the wild has taken it over, and the path is the way to see it, slowly, and quietly, and at the pace of a walk.

The seasons change the canal, as they change everything, and the walker who walks it through the year knows it in all its moods. In spring, the canal is bright, with the new leaves, and the first flowers, and the water is clear, and the boats are out, and the path is busy. In summer, the canal is green, with the weed, and the shade, and the water is warm, and the dragonflies are out, and the path is dusty. In autumn, the canal is golden, with the leaves falling into the water, and the light going low, and the path is soft with leaves. In winter, the canal is grey, and still, and the water is cold, and the path is muddy, and the walker is alone, and the canal is at its most itself.

The boats that use the canal now are not the working boats of its age. They are pleasure boats, long and narrow, painted in bright colours, with names, and with flower boxes, and with smoke from their stoves, and they move slowly, at the pace of a walk, and their crews wave, and the walker waves back, and the canal, which was built for freight, has become a place for leisure, and the leisure is slow, and it is the canal's second life, and it suits it.

The towpath is the walker's road, and it is a road with a history, and the history is written in its wear. The path was made by the horses, walking, day after day, year after year, pulling the boats, and the horses are gone, and the boats are gone, and the path remains, and the walkers use it, and the path is the canal's gift to the walker, a flat, quiet, waterside walk, through the country, and through the town, and the walker, on it, is walking the canal's history, step by step.

The builders of the canal were the navvies, and they deserve a paragraph, because the canal was dug by hand, and the hands were theirs. The navvies were labourers, who came from all over, and who lived in huts, beside the works, and who dug, with spades, and with barrows, and with gunpowder, where the ground was hard, and they dug for years, and they were paid by the yard, and they were strong, and they were rough, and they built the canal, and the locks, and the bridges, and the aqueducts, and they are forgotten, mostly, except in the names of the places they worked, and in the canal itself, which is their monument, and which they dug, by hand, out of the earth.

The railway came, and the canal declined, and the decline is part of the canal's story, and it is worth telling, because it is the story of a new machine replacing an old one. The railway was faster, and it could carry more, and it could go where the canal could not, and the freight left the canal, and the boats rotted, and the locks fell into ruin, and the towpath grew over, and the canal was forgotten, for a generation, until the people who loved it began to restore it, and the restoration is the canal's second life, and it is the life the walker walks in now.

The mileposts are the canal's furniture, and they are small, and cast in iron, and they stand beside the towpath, and they say how far it is to the next town, and to the last, and they are painted, black and white, and they are the canal's signs, and the walker, reading them, knows where they are, and how far they have come, and the mileposts are the canal's memory, kept in iron, beside the path.

The wharf is the canal's town, in miniature, and it is where the boats loaded, and unloaded, and where the warehouses stood, and where the coal was stacked, and the timber, and the grain, and the wharf is now, often, a place of houses, and of cafes, and of boats, and the warehouses are flats, and the cranes are gone, and the wharf is the canal's past, kept in brick, beside the water, and the walker, passing it, sees the town the canal made, and the town the canal left behind.

The horse is gone from the towpath, but the towpath remembers it, in its width, and in its wear, and in the low bridges, built for the horse to pass under, and in the tunnels, where the horse went over the top, while the boat went through, and the horse's path is the walker's path, and the walker, on it, walks where the horse walked, pulling the boat, at the pace of a walk, and the pace is the canal's pace, and it has not changed, in two hundred years.

The water in the canal does not come from the canal. It comes from the reservoirs, and the feeders, the streams, and the springs, that were built to supply it, and the reservoirs are lakes, in the hills, and the feeders are small channels, and the water is let into the canal, at the top, and it flows down, through the locks, and out, at the bottom, and the canal is a river that has been made, and the making of it, and the keeping of it, is the work of the canal, and the work goes on, year after year, by the people who keep it.

The restoration is the canal's newest chapter, and it is written by volunteers, who clear the locks, and repair the bridges, and dredge the channel, and rebuild the towpath, and who do it because the canal matters, and because the canal is worth keeping, and because the canal, restored, is a place for boats, and for walkers, and for wildlife, and for the town, and the restoration is slow, and it is the work of years, and it is the work of people who love the canal, and who will not let it die.

And the night on the canal is the canal at its quietest, and its most itself, when the boats are moored, and the lamps are lit, and the water is still, and the stars are out, and the walker, who has stayed late, hears the water, and the owls, and the silence, and the canal, in the dark, is a place of peace, and the peace is the canal's gift, to anyone who walks it, slowly, and looks, and listens.

The walk ends, as all walks do, at a bridge, or a lock, or a town, and the walker climbs up, from the towpath, to the road, and looks back, at the canal, and at the water, and at the path, and the canal goes on, in a straight line, to the horizon, and the walker goes home, with the sound of the water, and the sight of the kingfisher, and the smell of the damp, and the knowledge of the canal, and the walk, and the day. The canal is a ditch, dug by hand, and filled with water, and it is a wonder, and it is worth walking, slowly, and looking, and stopping, and the old canal path is the way to do it, one step at a time, beside the water, at the pace of a walk.

Demo program

# Demo: Walking the Old Canal Path
TEXT = "Walking the Old Canal Path\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nThe canal in this article is an illustrative one, drawn from the ordinary features of old canals in many places. No single real canal is described.\n\nThe old canal path begins at the bridge, where the road crosses the water, and where the towpath drops down, beside the canal, and the walking begins. The path is narrow, and it is worn, and it is lined with grass, and with the occasional tree, and it runs beside the water, in a straight line, for as far as the eye can see, and the walking is easy, because the canal is flat, and the path is flat, and the only work is the walking itself, and the looking.\n\nThe canal was built for boats, and the boats were built for the canal, and the two together were the freight of their age. The canal is a ditch, dug by hand, and lined with clay, and filled with water, and the boats that used it were long, and narrow, and loaded with coal, and with stone, and with grain, and with timber, and they were pulled by horses, along the towpath, and the horses walked, and the boats followed, at the pace of a walk, and the goods of the country moved, slowly, and steadily, along the water.\n\nThe locks are the canal's stairs, and they are the most interesting thing on the path. A lock is a chamber, with gates at each end, and the chamber is filled, or emptied, to raise a boat, or to lower it, from one level of the canal to the next. The gates are heavy, and they are worked by hand, by a beam, pushed by a person, and the water is let in, or let out, by a paddle, wound up by a windlass, and the boat rises, or falls, slowly, in the chamber, and the person at the gates watches it, and the watching is the work, and the work is slow, and it is the reason the canal moves at the pace of a walk.\n\nThe bridges are the canal's crossings, and they are small, and arched, and made of brick, and they carry the lanes, and the footpaths, over the water, and they are numbered, and the numbers are painted on them, in white, and the numbers are the canal's milestones, and the walker, counting them, knows how far they have come, and how far they have to go. The bridges are also the canal's roofs, in a sense, because the boats pass under them, and the horses pass over them, and the towpath passes beside them, and the whole of the canal's life passes through them, in one way or another.\n\nThe aqueduct is the canal's boldest piece, and it is the place where the canal crosses a valley, or a river, on a bridge of its own, carrying the water, in a trough, high above the ground, and the walker, crossing it, looks down, and sees the valley below, and the river, and the road, and the canal, above them all, a river in the sky, held up by arches, and by iron, and by the confidence of the men who built it. The aqueduct is a wonder, and it is a wonder that has stood for two hundred years, carrying water, and boats, and walkers, above the valley, as if it were the most natural thing in the world.\n\nThe tunnels are the canal's secret places, and they are dark, and long, and wet, and the boats that used them were pulled through by men, lying on their backs, on the roof of the boat, pushing against the roof of the tunnel with their feet, in the dark, in a practice called legging, and the leggers worked in the dark, and in the wet, and they were paid by the boat, and they were strong, and they were the canal's hidden workforce. The tunnels are closed now, to boats, and the towpath goes over them, or around them, and the walker, passing the tunnel mouth, looks into the dark, and imagines the leggers, and the boats, and the dark, and the water.\n\nThe wildlife of the canal is its own, and it is the reason the path is worth walking. The kingfisher flashes blue, along the water, and the heron stands, still, in the shallows, and the moorhens paddle, in the reeds, and the dragonflies hover, over the water, in the summer, and the fish rise, in the evening, and the canal, which was built for boats, has become a home for the wild, and the wild has taken it over, and the path is the way to see it, slowly, and quietly, and at the pace of a walk.\n\nThe seasons change the canal, as they change everything, and the walker who walks it through the year knows it in all its moods. In spring, the canal is bright, with the new leaves, and the first flowers, and the water is clear, and the boats are out, and the path is busy. In summer, the canal is green, with the weed, and the shade, and the water is warm, and the dragonflies are out, and the path is dusty. In autumn, the canal is golden, with the leaves falling into the water, and the light going low, and the path is soft with leaves. In winter, the canal is grey, and still, and the water is cold, and the path is muddy, and the walker is alone, and the canal is at its most itself.\n\nThe boats that use the canal now are not the working boats of its age. They are pleasure boats, long and narrow, painted in bright colours, with names, and with flower boxes, and with smoke from their stoves, and they move slowly, at the pace of a walk, and their crews wave, and the walker waves back, and the canal, which was built for freight, has become a place for leisure, and the leisure is slow, and it is the canal's second life, and it suits it.\n\nThe towpath is the walker's road, and it is a road with a history, and the history is written in its wear. The path was made by the horses, walking, day after day, year after year, pulling the boats, and the horses are gone, and the boats are gone, and the path remains, and the walkers use it, and the path is the canal's gift to the walker, a flat, quiet, waterside walk, through the country, and through the town, and the walker, on it, is walking the canal's history, step by step.\n\nThe builders of the canal were the navvies, and they deserve a paragraph, because the canal was dug by hand, and the hands were theirs. The navvies were labourers, who came from all over, and who lived in huts, beside the works, and who dug, with spades, and with barrows, and with gunpowder, where the ground was hard, and they dug for years, and they were paid by the yard, and they were strong, and they were rough, and they built the canal, and the locks, and the bridges, and the aqueducts, and they are forgotten, mostly, except in the names of the places they worked, and in the canal itself, which is their monument, and which they dug, by hand, out of the earth.\n\nThe railway came, and the canal declined, and the decline is part of the canal's story, and it is worth telling, because it is the story of a new machine replacing an old one. The railway was faster, and it could carry more, and it could go where the canal could not, and the freight left the canal, and the boats rotted, and the locks fell into ruin, and the towpath grew over, and the canal was forgotten, for a generation, until the people who loved it began to restore it, and the restoration is the canal's second life, and it is the life the walker walks in now.\n\nThe mileposts are the canal's furniture, and they are small, and cast in iron, and they stand beside the towpath, and they say how far it is to the next town, and to the last, and they are painted, black and white, and they are the canal's signs, and the walker, reading them, knows where they are, and how far they have come, and the mileposts are the canal's memory, kept in iron, beside the path.\n\nThe wharf is the canal's town, in miniature, and it is where the boats loaded, and unloaded, and where the warehouses stood, and where the coal was stacked, and the timber, and the grain, and the wharf is now, often, a place of houses, and of cafes, and of boats, and the warehouses are flats, and the cranes are gone, and the wharf is the canal's past, kept in brick, beside the water, and the walker, passing it, sees the town the canal made, and the town the canal left behind.\n\nThe horse is gone from the towpath, but the towpath remembers it, in its width, and in its wear, and in the low bridges, built for the horse to pass under, and in the tunnels, where the horse went over the top, while the boat went through, and the horse's path is the walker's path, and the walker, on it, walks where the horse walked, pulling the boat, at the pace of a walk, and the pace is the canal's pace, and it has not changed, in two hundred years.\n\nThe water in the canal does not come from the canal. It comes from the reservoirs, and the feeders, the streams, and the springs, that were built to supply it, and the reservoirs are lakes, in the hills, and the feeders are small channels, and the water is let into the canal, at the top, and it flows down, through the locks, and out, at the bottom, and the canal is a river that has been made, and the making of it, and the keeping of it, is the work of the canal, and the work goes on, year after year, by the people who keep it.\n\nThe restoration is the canal's newest chapter, and it is written by volunteers, who clear the locks, and repair the bridges, and dredge the channel, and rebuild the towpath, and who do it because the canal matters, and because the canal is worth keeping, and because the canal, restored, is a place for boats, and for walkers, and for wildlife, and for the town, and the restoration is slow, and it is the work of years, and it is the work of people who love the canal, and who will not let it die.\n\nAnd the night on the canal is the canal at its quietest, and its most itself, when the boats are moored, and the lamps are lit, and the water is still, and the stars are out, and the walker, who has stayed late, hears the water, and the owls, and the silence, and the canal, in the dark, is a place of peace, and the peace is the canal's gift, to anyone who walks it, slowly, and looks, and listens.\n\nThe walk ends, as all walks do, at a bridge, or a lock, or a town, and the walker climbs up, from the towpath, to the road, and looks back, at the canal, and at the water, and at the path, and the canal goes on, in a straight line, to the horizon, and the walker goes home, with the sound of the water, and the sight of the kingfisher, and the smell of the damp, and the knowledge of the canal, and the walk, and the day. The canal is a ditch, dug by hand, and filled with water, and it is a wonder, and it is worth walking, slowly, and looking, and stopping, and the old canal path is the way to do it, one step at a time, beside the water, at the pace of a walk.\n"

Full text

A Conversation with a Fictional Cartographer By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

This interview is a work of imagination. The interviewee, Mara Ellison, is a fictional character, and the interview is invented. No real person is interviewed here, and nothing in it is presented as a real conversation. It is a way of exploring, through a made-up voice, what it means to draw a map.

Q: You draw maps of places that do not exist. Why?

A: Because the places that do not exist are the ones that need maps most. A real place has been mapped a hundred times, by a hundred people, and the map is a copy of a copy. An imaginary place has never been mapped, and the map is the first time anyone has seen it. I draw the coast, and the coast becomes real, in the drawing, and the person who looks at the drawing can walk the coast, in their mind, and can see the cliffs, and the bays, and the headlands, and the map is the place, for them, as much as any real coast is a place.

Q: Where do you begin, when you begin a map?

A: I begin with the coast, always. The coast is the edge of the world, and the edge is the first thing a map needs. I draw the line of the shore, with its bays and its headlands, and I do not draw it straight, because no coast is straight, and I do not draw it smooth, because no coast is smooth. I draw it as if it had been made by the sea, over a long time, with the sea taking a bite here, and leaving a beach there, and the coast, when it is done, looks like a coast, because it was drawn the way a coast is made: slowly, and unevenly, and with accidents.

Q: And the land, behind the coast?

A: The land follows the coast. The mountains go where the coast is high, and the rivers go where the land is low, and the rivers run to the sea, because rivers always run to the sea, and the towns go where the rivers meet the sea, because towns always grow where the water is, and the roads go between the towns, and the forests go where the land is steep, and the fields go where the land is flat. A map is not a picture. It is a set of reasons, and the reasons must be right, or the map will look wrong, even to a person who has never seen the place.

Q: Do you invent the names, too?

A: The names are the hardest part, and the most important. A place without a name is not a place. It is a shape. The name makes it a place, and the name must sound like the place it names. A mountain has a hard name, and a river has a flowing name, and a town has a name that sounds like people live there. I keep a book of names, collected from old maps, and from books, and from the signs on the road, and I choose from it, and I change the names, a little, so that they are mine, and so that the place is mine, and the map, when it is done, has names that belong to it, and to nothing else.

Q: What tools do you use?

A: A pencil, and paper, and ink, and a ruler, and a compass, and patience. The pencil is for the first drawing, and the ink is for the second, and the ruler is for the straight lines, of which there are few, because few things in a landscape are straight, and the compass is for the circles, of which there are fewer, and the patience is for the whole of it, because a map takes days, and the days are long, and the work is slow, and the slowness is the point. A map made quickly looks quickly made, and a map made slowly looks as if it had always been there.

Q: Have you ever drawn a real place?

A: I have, and it is a different work. A real place must be measured, and the measurement must be right, and the map must agree with the ground, and the ground will prove the map wrong, if the map is wrong. An imaginary place must only agree with itself, and the agreement is harder, in a way, because there is no ground to check it against, and the map must be its own proof, and its own ground. I prefer the imaginary places, because the map is the place, and the place is the map, and there is no argument between them.

Q: What is the biggest mistake a mapmaker can make?

A: To draw a map that is beautiful, and wrong. A beautiful map that is wrong is worse than an ugly map that is right, because the beautiful map will be believed, and the belief will lead someone astray. The mapmaker's first duty is to be true, and the truth, in a map, is the shape of the coast, and the course of the river, and the place of the town, and the height of the mountain, and the beauty is the second duty, and it must serve the first, and not the other way around.

Q: Do you ever get lost in your own maps?

A: Every day. I draw a road, and I follow it, in my mind, to the town at its end, and I wonder what the town is like, and who lives there, and what they do, and I draw a little more, a market, and a square, and a church, and the town becomes real, to me, and I am lost in it, and I am glad to be lost, because being lost in a map is the best kind of being lost, and the map is the place where I am lost, and found, at the same time.

Q: What would you say to a person who wants to draw a map?

A: Begin with the coast, and draw it as if the sea had made it, and let the land follow, and let the rivers run to the sea, and let the towns grow where the rivers meet it, and give the places names that sound like themselves, and work slowly, and check your work, and do not be afraid to rub out, and to begin again, and remember that the map is not the place, but it is the way we see the place, and the way we remember it, and the way we find our way back to it.

Q: Do maps change the way people remember a place?

A: They do, and it is one of the map's quiet powers. A person who has visited a place will remember it as they saw it, in pieces, and in moments. A person who has seen the map of the place will remember it as a whole, as a shape, and the shape will stay with them, and the place, when they think of it, will be the shape on the map. The map becomes the memory, and the memory becomes the map, and the two are hard to tell apart, after a time. That is why a good map matters: because it is not only a guide to a place, but a way of remembering it.

Q: What about the maps on the screen, the ones that know where you are?

A: They are wonderful, and they are useful, and they are not maps, in the sense that I mean. A screen map shows you where you are, and where to go, and it does it well, and it has its place. But it does not show you the place. It shows you a route, and a dot, and the route is the thing, and the place is the background. My maps show the place, and the route is a small part of it. The screen map is a servant, and a good one. My map is a portrait, and a portrait is a different thing from a servant, and both are needed, and neither replaces the other.

Q: Is there a map you dream of drawing, and have not yet drawn?

A: There is. It is an atlas, of a whole world, with continents, and oceans, and islands, and every place in it named, and every river drawn, and every mountain placed, and the atlas would take a year, or more, and I have not begun it, because to begin it is to promise a year, and the year is a long promise, and I am not yet ready to make it. But the atlas is there, in my mind, and it grows, a little, every day, and one day I will begin it, with the coast, as always, and the coast will be the edge of the world, and the world will follow.

Q: What is the worst mistake you have made, in a map?

A: I once drew a river that ran uphill, and I did not notice it, for a week, and when I noticed it, I had to rub out a whole valley, and draw it again, and the rubbing out took longer than the drawing, and the lesson stayed with me: that a map must obey the land, even an imaginary land, and that the land has its rules, and the rules are not mine to break. The river runs downhill, always, and the map that forgets it is a map that is wrong, and the wrongness will be seen, by anyone who looks, even if they cannot say why.

Q: Do people use your maps, or only look at them?

A: Both, and the using is the best of it. A person will take one of my maps, and will plan a journey on it, in their mind, and will travel, in their mind, from the coast, to the town, to the mountains, and the journey is real, to them, and the map has done its work. Another person will hang a map on the wall, and will look at it, every day, and will dream of the place, and the dreaming is the using, too, in its own way. A map that is only looked at is a picture. A map that is used, even in the mind, is a map.

Q: What is in the border of your maps, the frame around the edge?

A: The border is the map's manners. It holds the map together, and it tells the person looking at it where the map begins, and where it ends, and it carries the compass rose, which says which way is north, and the scale, which says how far a finger's width is, in the world of the map, and the title, which says what the map is, and whose it is. The border is not decoration. It is the map's promise, that the world inside it is orderly, and that it can be understood, and that the understanding begins at the edge, and works inward.

Q: What does a map leave out?

A: Everything that cannot be drawn, which is most of the world. A map leaves out the smell of the rain, and the sound of the market, and the cold of the morning, and the warmth of the fire, and the people, and their stories, and their quarrels, and their loves. A map is a thin thing, a line, and a name, and a colour, and the world is thick, and the map knows it is thin, and the mapmaker knows it, and the map is honest about it, because it does not pretend to be the world. It pretends only to be a way of finding your way through the world, and that is enough, and it is all a map can do, and all it should try to do.

Q: And what is a map, in the end?

A: A map is a promise. It promises that the world can be understood, and that it can be held, in the hand, on a piece of paper, and that the understanding will help you to travel, and to find your way, and to come home. A map of a real place promises that the ground is as it is drawn. A map of an imaginary place promises that the imagination can be a place, and that the place can be visited, and that the visit is worth making. Both promises are worth keeping, and both are kept by drawing, slowly, and truly, and with patience, one line at a time.

# Demo: A Conversation with a Fictional Cartographer
TEXT = "A Conversation with a Fictional Cartographer\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nThis interview is a work of imagination. The interviewee, Mara Ellison, is a fictional character, and the interview is invented. No real person is interviewed here, and nothing in it is presented as a real conversation. It is a way of exploring, through a made-up voice, what it means to draw a map.\n\nQ: You draw maps of places that do not exist. Why?\n\nA: Because the places that do not exist are the ones that need maps most. A real place has been mapped a hundred times, by a hundred people, and the map is a copy of a copy. I draw the coast, and the coast becomes real, in the drawing, and the person who looks at the drawing can walk the coast, in their mind, and can see the cliffs, and the bays, and the headlands, and the map is the place, for them, as much as any real coast is a place.\n\nQ: Where do you begin, when you begin a map?\n\nA: I begin with the coast, always. The coast is the edge of the world, and the edge is the first thing a map needs. I draw the line of the shore, with its bays and its headlands, and I do not draw it straight, because no coast is straight, and I do not draw it smooth, because no coast is smooth. import re, math
words = len(TEXT.split())
sentences = len([s for s in re.split(r'[.!?]+', TEXT) if s.strip()])
paragraphs = len([p for p in TEXT.split(chr(10)+chr(10)) if p.strip()])
chars = len(TEXT)
reading_minutes = math.ceil(words / 200)
avg = words / sentences if sentences else 0
print(f"Article: A Conversation with a Fictional Cartographer")
print(f"Words: {words}")
print(f"Sentences: {sentences}")
print(f"Paragraphs: {paragraphs}")
print(f"Characters: {chars}")
print(f"Reading time (200 wpm): {reading_minutes} minutes")
print(f"Average words per sentence: {avg:.1f}")

Both promises are worth keeping, and both are kept by drawing, slowly, and truly, and with patience, one line at a time.\n"

the_count = len(re.findall(r'\bthe\b', TEXT, re.IGNORECASE))
