Full text

How a Lighthouse Lens Throws Light for Miles By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

A lighthouse throws its light for twenty miles, and it does so with a lamp that is no brighter than a lamp in a house. The secret is not the lamp. It is the lens, a great glass, cut into prisms, standing in the lantern room, gathering the light that would otherwise be wasted, and throwing it out to sea, in a beam, in a single direction, where the ships can see it. How the lens does this is a story about light, and about glass, and about a man who saw what others had missed.

Light travels in straight lines, and it spreads. A lamp in a room lights the whole room, because its light goes in every direction, and the light that goes up is wasted on the ceiling, and the light that goes down is wasted on the floor, and only a small part of it goes out of the window, to where it is needed. A lighthouse lamp, left on its own, would do the same: it would light the lantern room, and the sky above, and the sea below, and only a little of its light would go out to the horizon, where the ships are. The lens is the device that gathers the wasted light, and sends it where it is needed.

The lens is made of glass, and glass bends light. When light passes from air into glass, it changes direction, and when it passes from glass back into air, it changes direction again, and the change can be used, by shaping the glass, to bend the light to a purpose. A magnifying glass is a simple lens, thick in the middle, and thin at the edges, and it bends the light to a point, and the point is the focus, and the focus is where the light is gathered. The lighthouse lens is a magnifying glass, made large, and made clever, and it gathers the light of the lamp, and sends it out, in a beam, to the sea.

The cleverness is in the prisms. A solid lens, large enough to gather the light of a lighthouse lamp, would be enormously thick, and enormously heavy, and it would absorb much of the light it was meant to gather, and it would be impossible to make, and impossible to lift. The man who solved this, a Frenchman named Fresnel, saw that the lens did not need to be solid. It needed only its surfaces, the curves that bend the light, and the glass between them could be cut away, leaving a series of rings, each a prism, each bending the light as the solid lens would have bent it, but with a fraction of the glass, and a fraction of the weight. The Fresnel lens is a lens made of rings, and it is light, and it is efficient, and it is the reason lighthouses could be built as they were built.

The lens stands around the lamp, in a circle, and it is made of panels, each a set of prisms, and the panels are arranged so that the light from the lamp is gathered, above and below, and sent out, horizontally, in a beam. The beam is not a single ray. It is a fan of light, wide enough to be seen, and narrow enough to be bright, and it is the light that the ships see, flashing, as the lens turns. The lens turns, slowly, on a clockwork, or on a motor, and as it turns, the beam sweeps around the horizon, and the ship, far out at sea, sees the light appear, and disappear, and appear again, and the pattern of the appearing, the flash, is the lighthouse's signature, and the ship knows which lighthouse it is, by the flash, and by the colour, and by the period.

The flash is the lighthouse's language, and it is a language of light. Each lighthouse has its own flash, its own period, its own colour, and the mariner, with a book of lights, can tell which lighthouse is flashing, and where it is, and what it marks, and the flash is the lighthouse's name, written in light, on the dark sea. The lens makes the flash possible, because the lens makes the light bright enough to be seen, and the turning makes it flash, and the flash makes it known.

The lens must be kept clean, and it must be kept polished, because a dirty lens is a dim light, and a dim light is a broken promise. The keeper cleans the lens every day, with a cloth, and with care, and the cleaning is a ritual, and the ritual is the work, and the work is the light. The lens is glass, and glass is fragile, and the keeper handles it gently, and the gentleness is part of the craft, and the craft is the keeping of the light.

The lamp, too, has changed, over the years, from oil, to gas, to electricity, and the lens has stayed the same, because the lens does not care what the light is, only that it is there, to be gathered, and sent out. The oil lamp was a flame, and the gas lamp was a mantle, and the electric lamp is a bulb, and the lens gathers them all, and sends them out, in the same beam, to the same sea, and the ships see the same light, and the light is the same, because the lens is the same.

The lens is also a work of beauty, and it is worth saying so, because a lighthouse is a beautiful thing, and its beauty is in the lens, as much as in the tower. The lens, in the lantern room, catches the light, and throws it back, in prisms, and in colours, and the room is filled with light, and with rainbows, and the keeper, standing in it, is standing in a jewel, and the jewel is the lens, and the lens is the lighthouse, and the lighthouse is the light.

The lens turns, and the turning is done by a clockwork, a set of weights and gears, wound by the keeper, that drives the lens around, steadily, at the same speed, all night. The clockwork is a machine of patience, and it must be wound, and oiled, and kept, and its ticking is the sound of the lantern room, and the keeper knows its note, and knows, by a change in it, that something is wrong. The modern lens is turned by a motor, and the motor is silent, and the keeper, who remembers the clockwork, misses the ticking, and does not miss the winding, and the lens turns, either way, and the beam sweeps, and the ships see the flash.

In fog, the light is of little use, and the lighthouse must speak in another way. The fog signal is a sound, a horn, or a bell, or a siren, blown at intervals, and the intervals are the lighthouse's voice, in fog, as the flash is its voice, in clear weather. The lens cannot pierce the fog, because the fog scatters the light, and the light, scattered, goes nowhere, and the ships cannot see it. The sound travels further, in fog, than the light, and the lighthouse, in fog, is a sound, and not a light, and the keeper, in fog, tends the horn, as he tends the lamp, and the two together, light and sound, are the lighthouse's language, for all weathers.

The lens is large, and it is heavy, and its size is part of its wonder. A first-order lens, the largest kind, stands more than eight feet high, and weighs several tons, and it is made of hundreds of prisms, each cut, and polished, by hand, and fitted, into a frame of brass, and the frame is the lens's skeleton, and the prisms are its flesh, and the whole of it is a work of glass, and of brass, and of patience, and it was made, in a workshop, by men who never saw the sea, and who never saw the lighthouse it was made for, and who made it, to a pattern, and the pattern was the light.

The keepers who polished the lens were the lens's keepers, in the truest sense, and they polished it every day, with a cloth, and with whiting, and with care, and the polishing was a ritual, and the ritual was the work, and the work was the light. A lens that is not polished is a lens that is dim, and a dim lens is a broken promise, and the keepers knew it, and they polished, day after day, year after year, and the lens shone, and the light shone, and the ships saw it, and the sea was safe, because the lens was clean.

The modern lighthouse has a different lens, or no lens at all, and the change is worth a word, because it shows how the old gives way to the new. The electric lamp is bright, and the plastic lens is light, and the whole of it is smaller, and cheaper, and it does the work, and it does it well, and the great glass lens, the Fresnel lens, is retired, to a museum, where it stands, behind glass, and where people look at it, and wonder at it, and the wondering is right, because the lens is a wonder, and it is a wonder that worked, for a hundred years, and that saved ships, and lives, and the museum is a good place for it, and a sad one, because a lens in a museum throws no light to sea.

The physics of the lens is the physics of refraction, and it is worth a paragraph, because it is the heart of the whole thing. Light travels slower in glass than in air, and when it crosses the boundary, it bends, and the bending is called refraction, and the amount of the bending depends on the angle, and on the glass, and the lens is shaped so that the bending gathers the light, and sends it where it is needed. The prism is the simplest refractor, a wedge of glass, that bends the light by a fixed amount, and the Fresnel lens is a set of prisms, each bending the light by the amount needed, at its place, and the whole of them, together, bend the light as the solid lens would have, and the light goes out, in a beam, to the sea.

The lens and the tower belong together, and the tower deserves a word, because the lens is useless without the height. The tower lifts the lens above the rocks, and above the waves, and above the fog that lies low on the sea, and the height is what lets the light be seen for twenty miles, because the Earth is round, and the light, travelling straight, would meet the curve of the sea, and be hidden, if the lens were low. The tower is built of stone, or of iron, and it is built to stand, in the wind, and in the storm, and it is painted, white, or red, or banded, so that it can be seen by day, as the light can be seen by night, and the tower and the lens together are the lighthouse, and the lighthouse is the tower, and the light, and the two cannot be separated.

The light, when it leaves the lens, travels in a straight line, and it travels for twenty miles, and it is seen, by the ships, as a flash, on the horizon, and the flash is the lighthouse, saying: here I am, and here is the rock, and here is the shore, and steer clear, or steer for me, and the ships, hearing it, in light, steer accordingly, and the lighthouse, which is a tower, and a lamp, and a lens, has done its work, which is to throw light for miles, with a lamp no brighter than a lamp in a house, and a lens that gathers the light, and sends it where it is needed, out to sea, to the ships, in the dark.

The clockwork is a machine of patience, and it must be wound, and oiled, and kept, and its ticking is the sound of the lantern room, and the keeper knows its note, and knows, by a change in it, that something is wrong. The modern lens is turned by a motor, and the motor is silent, and the keeper, who remembers the clockwork, misses the ticking, and does not miss the winding, and the lens turns, either way, and the beam sweeps, and the ships see the flash.\n\nIn fog, the light is of little use, and the lighthouse must speak in another way. The fog signal is a sound, a horn, or a bell, or a siren, blown at intervals, and the intervals are the lighthouse's voice, in fog, as the flash is its voice, in clear weather. The lens cannot pierce the fog, because the fog scatters the light, and the light, scattered, goes nowhere, and the ships cannot see it. The sound travels further, in fog, than the light, and the lighthouse, in fog, is a sound, and not a light, and the keeper, in fog, tends the horn, as he tends the lamp, and the two together, light and sound, are the lighthouse's language, for all weathers.\n\nThe lens is large, and it is heavy, and its size is part of its wonder. A first-order lens, the largest kind, stands more than eight feet high, and weighs several tons, and it is made of hundreds of prisms, each cut, and polished, by hand, and fitted, into a frame of brass, and the frame is the lens's skeleton, and the prisms are its flesh, and the whole of it is a work of glass, and of brass, and of patience, and it was made, in a workshop, by men who never saw the sea, and who never saw the lighthouse it was made for, and who made it, to a pattern, and the pattern was the light.\n\nThe keepers who polished the lens were the lens's keepers, in the truest sense, and they polished it every day, with a cloth, and with whiting, and with care, and the polishing was a ritual, and the ritual was the work, and the work was the light. A lens that is not polished is a lens that is dim, and a dim lens is a broken promise, and the keepers knew it, and they polished, day after day, year after year, and the lens shone, and the light shone, and the ships saw it, and the sea was safe, because the lens was clean.\n\nThe modern lighthouse has a different lens, or no lens at all, and the change is worth a word, because it shows how the old gives way to the new. The electric lamp is bright, and the plastic lens is light, and the whole of it is smaller, and cheaper, and it does the work, and it does it well, and the great glass lens, the Fresnel lens, is retired, to a museum, where it stands, behind glass, and where people look at it, and wonder at it, and the wondering is right, because the lens is a wonder, and it is a wonder that worked, for a hundred years, and that saved ships, and lives, and the museum is a good place for it, and a sad one, because a lens in a museum throws no light to sea.\n\nThe physics of the lens is the physics of refraction, and it is worth a paragraph, because it is the heart of the whole thing. Light travels slower in glass than in air, and when it crosses the boundary, it bends, and the bending is called refraction, and the amount of the bending depends on the angle, and on the glass, and the lens is shaped so that the bending gathers the light, and sends it where it is needed. The prism is the simplest refractor, a wedge of glass, that bends the light by a fixed amount, and the Fresnel lens is a set of prisms, each bending the light by the amount needed, at its place, and the whole of them, together, bend the light as the solid lens would have, and the light goes out, in a beam, to the sea.\n\nThe lens and the tower belong together, and the tower deserves a word, because the lens is useless without the height. The tower lifts the lens above the rocks, and above the waves, and above the fog that lies low on the sea, and the height is what lets the light be seen for twenty miles, because the Earth is round, and the light, travelling straight, would meet the curve of the sea, and be hidden, if the lens were low. The tower is built of stone, or of iron, and it is built to stand, in the wind, and in the storm, and it is painted, white, or red, or banded, so that it can be seen by day, as the light can be seen by night, and the tower and the lens together are the lighthouse, and the lighthouse is the tower, and the light, and the two cannot be separated.\n\nThe light, when it leaves the lens, travels in a straight line, and it travels for twenty miles, and it is seen, by the ships, as a flash, on the horizon, and the flash is the lighthouse, saying: here I am, and here is the rock, and here is the shore, and steer clear, or steer for me, and the ships, hearing it, in light, steer accordingly, and the lighthouse, which is a tower, and a lamp, and a lens, has done its work, which is to throw light for miles, with a lamp no brighter than a lamp in a house, and a lens that gathers the light, and sends it where it is needed, out to sea, to the ships, in the dark.\n"
import re, math
words = len(TEXT.split())
sentences = len([s for s in re.split(r'[.!?]+', TEXT) if s.strip()])
paragraphs = len([p for p in TEXT.split(chr(10)+chr(10)) if p.strip()])
reading_minutes = math.ceil(words / 200)
the_count = len(re.findall(r'\bthe\b', TEXT, re.IGNORECASE))
print("RECORD: JAH-PFWAS-000023")
print("KIND: publishable full work record")
print(f"Article: How a Lighthouse Lens Throws Light for Miles")

A fictional portrait of a blacksmith in an age of machines, and the craft he keeps at the end of a lane.

Full text

Portrait of a Blacksmith in an Age of Machines By JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)

This portrait is a work of imagination. The blacksmith in it is fictional, a composite drawn from the nature of the trade rather than a report on any one named person. No real blacksmith is described.

The forge stands at the end of a lane, in a stone building with a slate roof, and a chimney that smokes, on the days when the fire is lit, and the fire is lit on most days, because the blacksmith works most days, and the work needs the fire. The blacksmith is a man of fifty, with arms like the branches of a tree, and hands that are scarred, and a face that is blackened, at the end of the day, with soot, and he is not a large man, but he is a strong one, and the strength is in his arms, and in his back, and in his patience, which is the blacksmith's greatest tool.

The forge is a simple place, and it is a place of fire, and of iron, and of water, and of air. The fire is in the hearth, fed by coal, and blown by the bellows, and the bellows are worked by the blacksmith, or by his apprentice, and the air makes the fire hot, and the heat makes the iron soft, and the soft iron is worked, on the anvil, with a hammer, and the hammer is the blacksmith's voice, and the anvil is his table, and the water is his quench, and the quench is the hardening, and the whole of the forge is a circle: fire, and iron, and hammer, and water, and fire again.

The work is the making of things, and the things are the things a town needs, and has always needed: the hinges for a door, and the latch for a gate, and the shoe for a horse, and the tool for a farmer, and the hook for a kitchen, and the rail for a stair, and the blacksmith makes them, one by one, by hand, in the fire, and on the anvil, and the making is slow, and it is exact, and it is the work of a craftsman, and the craftsman is the blacksmith, and the blacksmith is the forge.

The horseshoe is the blacksmith's best-known work, and it is worth a paragraph, because it shows the craft at its finest. The shoe is made from a bar of iron, heated, and bent, and shaped, on the anvil, to the shape of the horse's hoof, and the holes are punched, for the nails, and the shoe is fitted, to the hoof, hot, and the hot shoe is pressed to the hoof, and the hoof smokes, and the fit is checked, and the shoe is nailed on, and the horse stands, shod, and the blacksmith stands back, and the work is done, and the horse, which could not work without shoes, can work again, because the blacksmith made them.

The tools are the blacksmith's other work, and they are made for the hands that will use them. A hammer, for a mason, and a chisel, for a carpenter, and a hook, for a farmer, and a knife, for a cook, and each tool is made to the hand that will hold it, and to the work it will do, and the blacksmith, who makes them, knows the hands, and knows the work, and the tool, when it is made, fits the hand, and does the work, and the fit is the craft, and the craft is the blacksmith.

The machines have come, and the blacksmith knows it, and he does not pretend otherwise. The factory makes hinges, by the thousand, and shoes, by the thousand, and tools, by the thousand, and the factory's hinges are cheaper, and the factory's shoes are cheaper, and the factory's tools are cheaper, and the town buys them, because they are cheap, and the blacksmith's work has dwindled, year by year, to the work the factory cannot do: the repair, and the restoration, and the thing made for one person, and for one place, and the blacksmith does that work, and he does it well, and he knows that the work is smaller than it was, and that it will grow smaller still, and he does it anyway, because it is his work, and because it is worth doing.

The apprentice is the blacksmith's hope, and the apprentice is a young person, who has come to learn, and who stands at the bellows, and who watches, and who tries, and who fails, and who tries again, and the blacksmith teaches, by showing, and by letting the apprentice fail, and by correcting, gently, and the apprentice learns, slowly, that the iron must be hot enough, and not too hot, and that the hammer must fall true, and that the quench must be quick, and that the craft is in the hands, and in the eye, and in the patience, and the apprentice, in time, becomes a blacksmith, or does not, and the forge goes on, either way.

The fire is the heart of the forge, and it is lit in the morning, and it burns all day, and it is banked at night, and it is lit again in the morning, and the fire is the blacksmith's companion, and his master, and his tool, and the fire must be tended, and fed, and watched, and the watching is the work, and the work is the fire, and the fire is the forge.

The anvil is the blacksmith's table, and it is a great block of iron, on a stump of wood, and it rings, when the hammer strikes it, and the ring is the sound of the forge, and the sound is heard in the lane, and in the town, and the town knows, by the ring, that the blacksmith is at work, and that the forge is alive, and the ring is the blacksmith's signature, and it is heard, day after day, year after year.

The water is the quench, and it is in a barrel, beside the anvil, and the hot iron is plunged into it, and it hisses, and steams, and the iron hardens, and the hardening is the making, and the water is the blacksmith's temper, and the temper is the strength, and the strength is the tool, and the tool is the work.

The blacksmith's hands are his tools, and they are scarred, and burned, and strong, and they know the iron, by touch, and they know the heat, by the colour, and they know the hammer, by its weight, and the hands are the craft, and the craft is in the hands, and the hands are the blacksmith.

The farmer is the blacksmith's best customer, and the farmer's tools are the blacksmith's steadiest work. The ploughshare, worn thin by the earth, is brought to the forge, and the blacksmith heats it, and hammers it, and sharpens it, and the farmer takes it home, and ploughs with it, and brings it back, when it is worn again, and the cycle goes on, year after year, and the blacksmith and the farmer know each other, and the knowing is the business, and the business is the town.

The farrier's work is the shoeing, and it is a work apart, and it is done at the forge, or at the farm, with a portable forge, and the horse is held, and the hoof is cleaned, and pared, and the shoe is fitted, hot, and nailed, and the horse, which has stood, patiently, through it all, walks away, shod, and the farrier packs his tools, and goes to the next horse, and the next farm, and the shoeing is the blacksmith's travelling work, and it is the work that keeps the horses working, and the town moving.

The blacksmith's day begins early, with the lighting of the fire, and the fire is lit, and the bellows are worked, and the iron is put in, and the day's work is begun, and the work goes on, through the morning, and the afternoon, with a break for bread, and for tea, and the day ends, in the evening, with the banking of the fire, and the putting away of the tools, and the sweeping of the floor, and the blacksmith goes home, with his hands black, and his arms tired, and his day's work done, and the forge is quiet, until the morning.

The apprentice's first piece is a nail, and the nail is the beginning of the craft, and it is made from a rod of iron, heated, and drawn out, and headed, and cut, and the nail is small, and it is simple, and it is hard to make well, and the apprentice makes a hundred nails, and the hundred nails are the apprentice's education, and the education is in the hands, and in the eye, and in the patience, and the nail, when it is made well, is the proof that the apprentice is learning.

Wrought iron and cast iron are different things, and the blacksmith knows the difference, and works with the first, and not the second. Wrought iron is iron that has been worked, and it is tough, and it bends, and it does not break, and it is the blacksmith's metal, and it is worked, hot, on the anvil, and it takes the shape the blacksmith gives it. Cast iron is iron that has been poured, into a mould, and it is hard, and it is brittle, and it breaks, and it is the factory's metal, and it is not the blacksmith's, and the blacksmith, who knows the difference, works the wrought iron, and leaves the cast iron to the factory.

The forge is the village's meeting place, in its way, and it is where the men come, to talk, and to wait, while their work is done, and where the news is exchanged, and where the town's business is discussed, in the warmth of the fire, and the ring of the anvil, and the forge is a place of work, and of talk, and of company, and the blacksmith, who works there, is a part of the town, in a way that the factory, twenty miles away, never will be.

And the decline is the blacksmith's story, and it is told, in the forge, in the fewer horses, and the fewer orders, and the fewer apprentices, and the blacksmith, who has watched it, year by year, does not complain, but he knows, and he does the work that remains, and he does it well, and he teaches the apprentice, and he keeps the fire lit, and the forge goes on, because the work, though smaller, is still needed, and still worth doing, and the blacksmith is still there, at the end of the lane, with his fire, and his anvil, and his hammer, and his hands.

One day, the forge will close, and the fire will go out, and the anvil will be silent, and the lane will be quiet, and the town will have lost its blacksmith, and the loss will be felt, in the hinges that cannot be repaired, and the tools that cannot be made, and the horses that cannot be shod, and the town will remember, too late, that the blacksmith was not only a maker of things, but a keeper of a craft, and that the craft, once lost, is hard to regain, and that the forge, which stood at the end of the lane, was a place where fire, and iron, and a man's hands, made the things a town needs, one by one, by hand, in the fire, and on the anvil, and the memory of it will remain, in the ring of the anvil, and in the smoke of the chimney, and in the hands that learned the craft, and in the town that was served by it.

# Program: Portrait of a Blacksmith in an Age of Machines
TEXT = "Portrait of a Blacksmith in an Age of Machines\nBy JAH Signature (Justin Addam Higgins)\n\nThis portrait is a work of imagination. The blacksmith in it is fictional, a composite drawn from the nature of the trade rather than a report on any one named person. No real blacksmith is described.\n\nThe forge stands at the end of a lane, in a stone building with a slate roof, and a chimney that smokes, on the days when the fire is lit, and the fire is lit on most days, because the blacksmith works most days, and the work needs the fire. The blacksmith is a man of fifty, with arms like the branches of a tree, and hands that are scarred, and a face that is blackened, at the end of the day, with soot, and he is not a large man, but he is a strong one, and the strength is in his arms, and in his back, and in his patience, which is the blacksmith's greatest tool.\n\nThe forge is a simple place, and it is a place of fire, and of iron, and of water, and of air. The fire is in the hearth, fed by coal, and blown by the bellows, and the bellows are worked by the blacksmith, or by his apprentice, and the air makes the fire hot, and the heat makes the iron soft, and the soft iron is worked, on the anvil, with a hammer, and the hammer is the blacksmith's voice, and the anvil is his table, and the water is his quench, and the quench is the hardening, and the whole of the forge is a circle: fire, and iron, and hammer, and water, and fire again.\n\nThe work is the making of things, and the things are the things a town needs, and has always needed: the hinges for a door, and the latch for a gate, and the shoe for a horse, and the tool for a farmer, and the hook for a kitchen, and the rail for a stair, and the blacksmith makes them, one by one, by hand, in the fire, and on the anvil, and the making is slow, and it is exact, and it is the work of a craftsman, and the craftsman is the blacksmith, and the blacksmith is the forge.\n\nThe horseshoe is the blacksmith's best-known work, and it is worth a paragraph, because it shows the craft at its finest. The shoe is made from a bar of iron, heated, and bent, and shaped, on the anvil, to the shape of the horse's hoof, and the holes are punched, for the nails, and the shoe is fitted, to the hoof, hot, and the hot shoe is pressed to the hoof, and the hoof smokes, and the fit is checked, and the shoe is nailed on, and the horse stands, shod, and the blacksmith stands back, and the work is done, and the horse, which could not work without shoes, can work again, because the blacksmith made them.\n\nThe tools are the blacksmith's other work, and they are made for the hands that will use them. A hammer, for a mason, and a chisel, for a carpenter, and a hook, for a farmer, and a knife, for a cook, and each tool is made to the hand that will hold it, and to the work it will do, and the blacksmith, who makes them, knows the hands, and knows the work, and the tool, when it is made, fits the hand, and does the work, and the fit is the craft, and the craft is the blacksmith.\n\nThe machines have come, and the blacksmith knows it, and he does not pretend otherwise. The factory makes hinges, by the thousand, and shoes, by the thousand, and tools, by the thousand, and the factory's hinges are cheaper, and the factory's shoes are cheaper, and the factory's tools are cheaper, and the town buys them, because they are cheap, and the blacksmith's work has dwindled, year by year, to the work the factory cannot do: the repair, and the restoration, and the thing made for one person, and for one place, and the blacksmith does that work, and he does it well, and he knows that the work is smaller than it was, and that it will grow smaller still, and he does it anyway, because it is his work, and because it is worth doing.\n\nThe apprentice is the blacksmith's hope, and the apprentice is a young person, who has come to learn, and who stands at the bellows, and who watches, and who tries, and who fails, and who tries again, and the blacksmith teaches, by showing, and by letting the apprentice fail, and by correcting, gently, and the apprentice learns, slowly, that the iron must be hot enough, and not too hot, and that the hammer must fall true, and that the quench must be quick, and that the craft is in the hands, and in the eye, and in the patience, and the apprentice, in time, becomes a blacksmith, or does not, and the forge goes on, either way.\n\nThe fire is the heart of the forge, and it is lit in the morning, and it burns all day, and it is banked at night, and it is lit again in the morning, and the fire is the blacksmith's companion, and his master, and his tool, and the fire must be tended, and fed, and watched, and the watching is the work, and the work is the fire, and the fire is the forge.\n\nThe anvil is the blacksmith's table, and it is a great block of iron, on a stump of wood, and it rings, when the hammer strikes it, and the ring is the sound of the forge, and the sound is heard in the lane, and in the town, and the town knows, by the ring, that the blacksmith is at work, and that the forge is alive, and the ring is the blacksmith's signature, and it is heard, day after day, year after year.\n\nThe water is the quench, and it is in a barrel, beside the anvil, and the hot iron is plunged into it, and it hisses, and steams, and the iron hardens, and the hardening is the making, and the water is the blacksmith's temper, and the temper is the strength, and the strength is the tool, and the tool is the work.\n\nThe blacksmith's hands are his tools, and they are scarred, and burned, and strong, and they know the iron, by touch, and they know the heat, by the colour, and they know the hammer, by its weight, and the hands are the craft, and the craft is in the hands, and the hands are the blacksmith.\n\nThe farmer is the blacksmith's best customer, and the farmer's tools are the blacksmith's steadiest work. The ploughshare, worn thin by the earth, is brought to the forge, and the blacksmith heats it, and hammers it, and sharpens it, and the farmer takes it home, and ploughs with it, and brings it back, when it is worn again, and the cycle goes on, year after year, and the blacksmith and the farmer know each other, and the knowing is the business, and the business is the town.\n\nThe farrier's work is the shoeing, and it is a work apart, and it is done at the forge, or at the farm, with a portable forge, and the horse is held, and the hoof is cleaned, and pared, and the shoe is fitted, hot, and nailed, and the horse, which has stood, patiently, through it all, walks away, shod, and the farrier packs his tools, and goes to the next horse, and the next farm, and the shoeing is the blacksmith's travelling work, and it is the work that keeps the horses working, and the town moving.\n\nThe blacksmith's day begins early, with the lighting of the fire, and the fire is lit, and the bellows are worked, and the iron is put in, and the day's work is begun, and the work goes on, through the morning, and the afternoon, with a break for bread, and for tea, and the day ends, in the evening, with the banking of the fire, and the putting away of the tools, and the sweeping of the floor, and the blacksmith goes home, with his hands black, and his arms tired, and his day's work done, and the forge is quiet, until the morning.\n\nThe apprentice's first piece is a nail, and the nail is the beginning of the craft, and it is made from a rod of iron, heated, and drawn out, and headed, and cut, and the nail is small, and it is simple, and it is hard to make well, and the apprentice makes a hundred nails, and the hundred nails are the apprentice's education, and the education is in the hands, and in the eye, and in the patience, and the nail, when it is made well, is the proof that the apprentice is learning.\n\nWrought iron and cast iron are different things, and the blacksmith knows the difference, and works with the first, and not the second. Wrought iron is iron that has been worked, and it is tough, and it bends, and it does not break, and it is the blacksmith's metal, and it is worked, hot, on the anvil, and it takes the shape the blacksmith gives it. Cast iron is iron that has been poured, into a mould, and it is hard, and it is brittle, and it breaks, and it is the factory's metal, and it is not the blacksmith's, and the blacksmith, who knows the difference, works the wrought iron, and leaves the cast iron to the factory.\n\nThe forge is the village's meeting place, in its way, and it is where the men come, to talk, and to wait, while their work is done, and where the news is exchanged, and where the town's business is discussed, in the warmth of the fire, and the ring of the anvil, and the forge is a place of work, and of talk, and of company, and the blacksmith, who works there, is a part of the town, in a way that the factory, twenty miles away, never will be.\n\nAnd the decline is the blacksmith's story, and it is told, in the forge, in the fewer horses, and the fewer orders, and the fewer apprentices, and the blacksmith, who has watched it, year by year, does not complain, but he knows, and he does the work that remains, and he does it well, and he teaches the apprentice, and he keeps the fire lit, and the forge goes on, because the work, though smaller, is still needed, and still worth doing, and the blacksmith is still there, at the end of the lane, with his fire, and his anvil, and his hammer, and his hands.\n\nOne day, the forge will close, and the fire will go out, and the anvil will be silent, and the lane will be quiet, and the town will have lost its blacksmith, and the loss will be felt, in the hinges that cannot be repaired, and the tools that cannot be made, and the horses that cannot be shod, and the town will remember, too late, that the blacksmith was not only a maker of things, but a keeper of a craft, and that the craft, once lost, is hard to regain, and that the forge, which stood at the end of the lane, was a place where fire, and iron, and a man's hands, made the things a town needs, one by one, by hand, in the fire, and on the anvil, and the memory of it will remain, in the ring of the anvil, and in the smoke of the chimney, and in the hands that learned the craft, and in the town that was served by it.\n"
import re, math
words = len(TEXT.split())
sentences = len([s for s in re.split(r'[.!?]+', TEXT) if s.strip()])
paragraphs = len([p for p in TEXT.split(chr(10)+chr(10)) if p.strip()])
reading_minutes = math.ceil(words / 200)
the_count = len(re.findall(r'\bthe\b', TEXT, re.IGNORECASE))
print("RECORD: JAH-PFWAS-000024")
print("KIND: publishable full work record")
print(f"Article: Portrait of a Blacksmith in an Age of Machines")
print(f"Words: {words}")
print(f"Sentences: {sentences}")
print(f"Paragraphs: {paragraphs}")
print(f"Reading time (200 wpm): {reading_minutes} minutes")
print(f"Occurrences of the word 'the': {the_count}")
